The L p L^{p} Neumann problem for parabolic operators with coefficients satisfying small Carleson condition
Martin Dindoš, Linhan Li, Jill Pipher

Abstract
. In this paper, we resolve the question of whether the Neumann problem for the parabolic PDE − ∂ t u + div ( A ∇ u ) = 0 -\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0 on a Lipschitz cylinder 𝒪 × ℝ \mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R} is solvable for some p ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) p\in(1,\infty) under the assumption that the matrix A A is elliptic with bounded and measurable coefficients that satisfy a natural Carleson condition (a parabolic analog of the so-called DKP-condition). We prove that for any 1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty the Neumann problem is solvable under the assumption that both the Carleson norm of coefficients and the Lipschitz constant of the domain are sufficiently small (with dependence on p p ). The question of what happens in the “large Carleson norm/large Lipschitz constant” regime remains open, and even for elliptic PDEs this question has only been resolved in two dimensions. This paper complements results from our recent manuscript (by the same authors) in which the parabolic regularity problem has been fully resolved in both the small and large Carleson norm regime. Previously, the Dirichlet problem had been resolved under the same conditions by various authors.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 35K20, 35K10

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we present the first results on the solvability of the
Neumann problem in all dimensions for a class of parabolic operators in divergence form with
time-varying coefficients
satisfying a minimal smoothness assumption. It is assumed that the coefficients of the matrix (not necessarily symmetric) defining the operators under consideration satisfy a well-studied Carleson measure condition. When the norm of this measure is sufficiently small, we obtain nontangential maximal function estimates on the gradient of the solution when the Neumann data belongs to
L^{p}
, for all
1<p<\infty
.

Quantifying the regularity of these divergence form operators via a
Carleson measure condition on the coefficients
has a long history. This regularity condition on the coefficients arose from a
particular change of
variable that is relevant to the study of solutions to smooth elliptic or parabolic equations in Lipschitz domains (
[
5
,
27
]
). Dahlberg had conjectured that this condition gives rise to a “good” class (in terms of solvability of boundary value problems) of
elliptic operators. The conjecture, in the case of the Dirichlet problem, was resolved for elliptic equations in
[
23
]
and
[
17
]
and extended to parabolic equations in
[
9
]
and
[
18
]
.

A related boundary value problem, the Regularity problem, imposes one derivative of additional smoothness on the Dirichlet data, and asks for nontangential maximal function estimates on gradients of solutions. For elliptic operators, both the Regularity and Neumann problems were settled in
[
19
]
in the small Carleson norm regime. When the Carleson condition is not small, matters are much less well understood. It took a long time to settle the elliptic
Regularity problem in this regime - this was only accomplished in 2023 independently and simultaneously in
[
10
]
and (for very general domains) in
[
25
]
. In dimension two,
[
24
]
had observed that there is a special dual relationship between Neumann problems and Regularity problems within a class of operators. When the regularity assumptions on the operator are preserved under this duality, Neumann and Regularity are equivalent. This does not persist in higher dimensions.

Turning to the parabolic theory, the operators we consider here
have the form

(1.1)

\mathcal{L}u:=-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0\quad\text{in }\Omega,

and will be defined in a domain
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
where
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
or
\mathcal{O}
is a bounded or unbounded Lipschitz domain.

The matrix
A=[a_{ij}(X,t)]
is an
n\times n
matrix satisfying the uniform ellipticity condition with
X\in\mathcal{O}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
That is, there exist positive constants
\lambda
and
\Lambda
such that

(1.2)

\lambda|\xi|^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}(X,t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\Lambda|\xi|^{2}

for almost every
(X,t)\in\Omega
and all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The importance of understanding the behavior of solutions to parabolic operators with rough coefficients or in domains with rough boundaries has been evident since the work of Nash in
[
26
]
, which made it possible, and interesting, to study boundary value problems for equations with minimal smoothness.
Many of the earlier developments for operators in such settings have focused on the time-independent case,
A(X,t)=A(X)
, or on
scalar equations in time-varying domains. New difficulties arise when the matrix depends on the time variable as well, for example in trying to use
Fourier transform methods. Despite the obstacles, the time-varying parabolic Dirichlet problems have now been settled for the operators under consideration in this paper in both the small and large Carleson norm regimes.
The first results on solvability of the Dirichlet problem for the small Carleson norm regime were obtained in
[
11
]
, for

p\geq 2
, and improved to
p>1
in
[
9
]
. The solvability of the Dirichlet problem for the large Carleson norm regime was
established in
[
18
]
. For more history on solvability of parabolic boundary value problems with coefficients satisfying various mild regularity conditions, we refer the reader to the introductions of
[
9
]
and
[
8
]
.
Recently, adapting new ideas and methods introduced in
[
10
]
, the solvability of the Regularity problem for parabolic operators was obtained in
[
14
]
, with sharp results in both small and large norm regimes.

As in the elliptic setting, the parabolic Neumann problem is harder to understand and resolve. The only results we are aware of for non-smooth coefficients in the time-varying setting are in
[
2
]
. There, the authors imposed structural conditions on their matrices different from those considered here, namely, requiring independence of the transversal spatial variable, and other constraints. This in turn, built upon the prior results of
[
28
]
, where the additional condition of time independence was assumed on the matrices.
We also note that the parabolic Dirichlet and Regularity problem for transversally independent matrices without any additional assumptions on the coefficient matrix (beyond the natural ones: real, bounded measurable and elliptic) were resolved in
[
3
]
and
[
20
]
, respectively.

In this paper, our matrices are allowed to be non-symmetric and time-varying, thus obtaining the full parabolic analog of the elliptic Neumann results of

[
19
]
as well as complementing our study of the parabolic Regularity problem in
[
14
]
.
Hence the main results of this paper and of
[
14
]
bring the state of the art for the parabolic theory for time-varying matrices fully into alignment
with its elliptic counterpart.

Specifically for parabolic equations, we assume that
the measure defined by

(1.3)

d\mu={\sup_{B_{\delta(X,t)/2}(X,t)}}\left(\delta(Y,s)|\nabla A|^{2}+\delta(Y,s)^{3}|\partial_{t}A|^{2}\right)dX\,dt

is the density of a Carleson measure on
\Omega
with Carleson norm
\|\mu\|_{C}
, or alternatively we ask for

(1.4)

d\mu=\delta^{-1}(X,t)\left({\mathrm{osc}_{B_{\delta(X,t)/2}(X,t)}}A\right)^{2}dX\,dt,

to be the density of a Carleson measure with
\|\mu\|_{C}<\infty
. This is an alternative condition introduced in
[
19
]
. Note that (
1.3
) implies that

(1.5)

\delta(X,t)|\nabla A|+\delta(X,t)^{2}|\partial_{t}A|\leq K<\infty

with
K\lesssim\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}^{1/2}
.
Here and in the sequel,
\delta(X,t)
denotes the parabolic distance to the boundary of
\Omega
, which is defined as:

\delta(X,t)=\inf_{(Y,\tau)\in\partial\Omega}\left(\left|X-Y\right|^{2}+\left|t-\tau\right|\right)^{1/2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{osc}_{B}A=\sup_{(X,t),(Y,s)\in B}|A(X,t)-A(Y,s)|.

(See (
2.9
) in the next section for the definition of Carleson measures.)

Our main result establishes the solvability of the Neumann boundary value problem for
\mathcal{L}
when the Carleson norm,
\|\mu\|_{C}
, is sufficiently small (the
small Carleson condition
). Specifically, if the conormal derivative of the solution of
\mathcal{L}u=0
is prescribed and belongs to
L^{p}
, for any
1<p<\infty
, then we obtain nontangential maximal function estimates (suitably defined) in
L^{p}
on the gradient of the solution. Even in the elliptic setting, the solvability of the Neumann problem without the small Carleson assumption remains an open problem in dimensions larger than
2
.

Let us make some closer comparisons of our result with those of
[
14
]
and
[
19
]
.
In
[
14
]
, we obtained solvability of the Regularity problem when the matrix
A(X,t)
satisfied conditions (
1.3
) and (
1.5
), for boundary data in
L^{p}
, in a range of
p
dual to that of solvability of the Dirichlet problem for the adjoint operator. In particular, since the Dirichlet problem for such operators was solved for all
1<p<\infty
under the
small Carleson condition in
[
9
]
, this gives
solvability of the Regularity problem for all
1<p<\infty
for operators with small Carleson norm.
A key difference between these Regularity and Neumann boundary value problems
is that the boundary data for the parabolic Regularity problem includes a half derivative in time,
D^{1/2}_{t}f
, of the Dirichlet data, along with the spatial tangential derivatives. This is a further departure of the parabolic setting from the elliptic setting. We elaborate on this issue in the Appendix.

As alluded to earlier, in
[
19
]
,
the elliptic Regularity and Neumann problems were solved for boundary data in
L^{p}
for all
1<p<\infty
assuming that the Carleson norm,
\|\mu\|_{C}
, is sufficiently small.
However, to obtain this result in the parabolic setting, we cannot follow the elliptic roadmap precisely. In
[
19
]
, the solvability of the elliptic Regularity problem was proven first, providing a bound on the nontangential maximal function in terms of the tangential derivatives on the boundary. Thus the solvability of the Neumann problem reduced to the
L^{p}
equivalence of tangential and conormal derivatives on the boundary. But in the parabolic setting, the Regularity data involves a complicating (non-local) half derivative in time. Hence a different approach is required to obtain the nontangential estimates for our Neumann problem. We will derive the appropriate nontangential maximal function estimates by
making use of the so-called
p
-adapted square function
, which first appeared in

[
17
]
. Finally, a key component of the methodology for solving the Neumann problem in both the elliptic and parabolic settings is the discovery (first observed in
[
19
]
) that there is a useful PDE satisfied by the conormal derivative.

We now state the main theorem of this paper.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
with
\mathcal{O}
being a bounded or unbounded Lipschitz domain with Lipschitz character
(\ell,N,C_{0})
(see Definition
2.2
). Let
\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{t}+\divg(A\nabla\cdot)
be a parabolic operator on
\Omega
satisfying (
1.2
) and either (
1.3
) or (
1.4
). Let
p\in(1,\infty)
. Then there exists
\delta=\delta(\lambda,\Lambda,n,p)
such that if
\max\left\{\ell,\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}\right\}<\delta
, then the
L^{p}
Neumann problem
(N)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
for
\mathcal{L}
is solvable in
\Omega
.
In particular, for any
g\in L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
the solution
u
solving
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
with Neumann datum
g
satisfies the estimate

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C(\lambda,\Lambda,n,p)\|g\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

It remains to address the question of whether, in addition to the estimate above,
we also have nontangential maximal function estimates of
D^{1/2}_{t}u

Estimates of this type for the
L^{2}
Neumann problem have been shown in
[
2
]
in the upper half space. By
[
8
]
they are known for the Regularity problem. We discuss this question in detail in Appendix
A
, and the answer is rather surprising as it depends on whether
\mathcal{O}
is bounded or unbounded. We show that no estimate of this type exists if
\mathcal{O}
is bounded. However, when
\mathcal{O}
is an unbounded domain above a Lipschitz graph, and
H_{t}
denotes the Hilbert
transform in the variable
t
, the estimate

\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+\|\tilde{N}(H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C(\lambda,\Lambda,n,p)\|g\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)},

does hold for any
p\in(1,2]
whenever the
L^{p}
Neumann problem as in Theorem
1.6
with Neumann datum
g
is solvable.
We expect such an estimate to be true when
p>2
as well, but have not attempted to resolve it here.

1.1.
A guide to reading this paper

Following this introduction, Section
2
collects the necessary definitions and discusses the space of energy solutions where the Lax-Milgram method furnishes the weak solutions to the Neumann problem. In contrast to the elliptic setting, the Neumann data for these parabolic equations need not reside in a space modulo constants - that is, the data need not have mean value zero. This fact is the foundation of the novel observation (in Appendix
A
) that the further control of the half time derivative of solutions in terms of Neumann data is not possible in domains bounded in space.

In Section
3
, we present the logic of the proof of our main theorem, which requires a number of technical steps that are carried out in the subsequent three sections. In particular, most of these technical steps require an apriori finiteness assumption on the
L^{p}
norm of the nontangential maximal function of the gradient of the solution. A good deal of this section is taken up in establishing this a priori finiteness, assuming smoothness of the coefficients and of the data. It was surprising to us that these a priori estimates on solutions to “good equations” were not already a part of the literature.

The goal in our main theorem is the control of the nontangential maximal function of the gradient of the solution. Such estimates, for certain PDE, can often be achieved via
square function
estimates. The square function connects the solution to its boundary data via integration by parts using the PDE. For our result, we needed to make use of two different square functions, one of which is the
p
-adapted square function. It is crucial to establish mutual boundedness of these square functions with nontangential maximal functions, and new difficulties appear when these operators are applied to the gradient of a solution. At various points in the technical steps, we make use of a paradigm that was originally observed in the elliptic setting of
[
19
]
, but has additional complications here. This paradigm amounts to the observation that, in the setting of the upper space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
, the extension of the conormal derivative,
H=\sum_{j}a_{nj}\partial_{j}u
, satisfies an inhomogeneous PDE, with a right hand side that has good estimates in virtue of the (small) Carleson assumption on the coefficients
a_{ij}
. Our strategy for estimates of the (full) spatial gradient of the solution often proceeds by first establishing control of the tangential derivatives using the PDE they satisfy. Then we establish control of a term we denote by
H
which is a linear combination of derivatives that has the conormal derivative as its boundary values. Finally, using the combination of these two we control the transverse derivative
\partial_{n}u
.

In Section
4
, we derive the Caccioppoli inequalities for derivatives of solutions, and for
H
, making use of the PDE that
H
satisfies (and which was studied in
[
19
]
). In Section
5
, we prove that the
p
-adapted square function bounds the nontangential maximal function acting on gradients of solutions. In Section
6
we control the
p
-adapted square function, proving a key lemma (Lemma
6.24
). Finally, Section
7
proves a key estimate to complete the proof of our main theorem: the bound of the (usual) square function of the gradient of a solution in terms of the nontangential maximal function. Once again, the key strategy is to employ bounds for tangential derivatives of the solutions, and for
H
, coming from the equations that this combination of derivatives satisfies.

In Section
8
, we present the localization estimates required to establish solvability of the Neumann problem on bounded Lipschitz cylinders, bootstrapping from the solvability on unbounded domains.
The key idea is that we use Theorem
8.2
(proven in
[
15
]
) to deduce properties of solutions on bounded Lipschitz cylinders from already established solvability on unbounded domains. The second important step is the observation of Brown
[
4
]
that solutions of the Neumann problem whose boundary data is localized in time to a small interval
(t,t+r_{0}^{2})
do enjoy bounds such as (
8.14
) with small terms on the righthand side (depending on
r_{0}
) that can be absorbed in the bound. The final step allowing us to add up all localized data is the exponential decay of
\tilde{N}(\nabla u)
away from the Neumann support.

As mentioned earlier, Appendix
A
presents a novel dichotomy concerning control of the half time derivative of solutions to the Neumann problem depending on whether the domain is bounded or unbounded. On bounded domains, the Neumann data exerts no control on the half time derivative of the solution, but on unbounded domains, such control can be proven - at least for
L^{p}
data in the range
p\leq 2
.

2.
Definitions

Definition 2.1
.

{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an
\ell
-cylinder of diameter
d
if there
exists an orthogonal coordinate system
(x,x_{n})
with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

{\mathbb{Z}}=\{(x,x_{n})\;:\;|x|\leq d,\;-(\ell+1)d\leq x_{n}\leq(\ell+1)d\}

and for
s>0
,

s{\mathbb{Z}}:=\{(x,x_{n})\;:\;|x|\leq sd,-(\ell+1)sd\leq x_{n}\leq(\ell+1)sd\}.

Definition 2.2
.

\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Lipschitz domain with Lipschitz
‘character’
(\ell,N,C_{0})
if there exists a positive scale
r_{0}\in(0,\infty]
and
at most
N

\ell
-cylinders
\{{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
of diameter
d
, with

\frac{r_{0}}{C_{0}}\leq d\leq C_{0}r_{0}
such that

(i)
8{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}\cap{\partial\mathcal{O}}
is the graph of a Lipschitz
function
\phi_{j}
,
\|\nabla\phi_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq\ell\,;\phi_{j}(0)=0
,

(ii)
\displaystyle{\partial\mathcal{O}}=\bigcup_{j}({{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}\cap{\partial\mathcal{O}})
,

(iii)
\displaystyle{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}\cap\mathcal{O}\supset\left\{(x,x_{n})\in\mathcal{O}\;:\;|x|<d,\;\mathrm{dist}\left((x,x_{n}),{\partial\mathcal{O}}\right)\leq\frac{d}{2}\right\}
.

(iv) Each cylinder
\displaystyle{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}
contains points from
\mathcal{O}^{c}={\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus\mathcal{O}
.

(v) If
r_{0}<\infty
the domain
\mathcal{O}
is a bounded set.

Remark.
If the scale
r_{0}
is finite, then the domain
\mathcal{O}
from the definition above is a bounded Lipschitz domain. However, we shall also allow both
r_{0},\,d
to be infinite, and in this case, since
\displaystyle{{\mathbb{Z}}}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we have that
\mathcal{O}
can be written in some coordinate system as

(2.3)

\mathcal{O}=\{(x,x_{n}):x_{n}>\phi(x)\}\quad\mbox{ where $\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$ is a Lipschitz function.}

The set
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
will be called a parabolic Lipschitz cylinder with Lipschitz base
\mathcal{O}
.
We will need an extension of the definition of spaces
\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}
to Lipschitz cylinders.

Definition 2.4
.

A parabolic cube on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
centered at
(X,t)
with sidelength
r
is defined as

Q_{r}(X,t):=\{(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:|x_{i}-y_{i}|<r\ \text{ for }1\leq i\leq n,\ |t-s|^{1/2}<r\}.

When writing a lowercase point
(x,t)
, we mean a boundary parabolic cube on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
,
which has an analogous definition but in one less spatial dimension:

(2.5)

Q_{r}(x,t):=\{(y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:|x_{i}-y_{i}|<r\ \text{ for }1\leq i\leq n-1,\ |t-s|^{1/2}<r\}.

A parabolic ball on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
centered at
(X,t)
with radius
r
is the ball

(2.6)

B_{r}(X,t):=\{(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:\|(X-Y,t-s)\|<r\},

where
\|(\xi,\tau)\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
is defined as
the unique positive solution
\rho
to the following equation

(2.7)

\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{\rho^{4}}=1.

Recall that
\|\cdot\|
scales as the parabolic distance function

d_{p}((X,t),(Y,s)):=\left(\left|X-Y\right|^{2}+\left|t-s\right|\right)^{1/2}\sim\|(X-Y,t-s)\|.

For parabolic balls at the boundary we use notation
\Delta_{r}(X,t)=B_{r}(X,t)\cap\partial\Omega
. In the special case
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
we drop the last coordinate and also write
\Delta_{r}(x,t)
with understanding that the ball is centered at
(X,t)=(x,0,t)
.

We sometimes use the notation
B_{X,t}:=B_{\delta(X,t)/2}(X,t)
for
(X,t)\in\Omega
.

We take this opportunity to define (parabolic) Carleson measures.

Definition 2.8
.

A measure
\mu
is a Carleson measure on
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
if there exists a constant
M
such that for
(X,t)\in\partial\Omega
and

0<r<\diam(\Omega)
,

(2.9)

\mu(B_{r}(X,t)\cap\Omega)\leq Mr^{n+1}.

The infimum over all
M
such that (
2.9
) holds is the Carleson norm,
\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}
, of
\mu
.

Definition 2.10
.

For
a>0
and
(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega
, unless otherwise defined, we denote the nontangential parabolic cones by

(2.11)

\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau):=\left\{(X,t)\in\Omega:d_{p}((X,t),(q,\tau))<(1+a)\delta(X,t)\right\},

and
\delta(\cdot)
is the parabolic distance to the boundary:

\delta(X,t)=\inf_{(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega}d_{p}((X,t),(q,\tau)).

We also use the truncated parabolic cones: for
r>0
,

\Gamma_{a}^{r}(q,\tau):=\left\{(X,t)\in\Omega:d_{p}((X,t),(q,\tau))<(1+a)\delta(X,t),\,\delta(X,t)<r\right\}

is the parabolic cone with vertex
(q,\tau)
truncated at height
r
.

Definition 2.12
.

For
w\in L^{\infty}_{\loc}(\Omega)
, we define the nontangential maximal function of
w
as

N_{a}(w)(q,\tau):=\sup_{(X,t)\in\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}\left|w(X,t)\right|\quad\text{for }(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega.

If
w\in L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega)
,
p\in(0,\infty)
, we need the modified nontangential maximal function

(2.13)

\widetilde{N}_{a,p}(w)(q,\tau):=\sup_{(X,t)\in\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{\delta(X,t)/2}(X,t)}\left|w(Y,s)\right|^{p}dYds\right)^{1/p}\quad\text{for }(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega,

where
B_{r}(X,t)
is a parabolic ball centered at
(X,t)
with “radius”
r
, that is,

B_{r}(X,t):=\left\{(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:d_{p}((Y,s),(X,t))<r\right\}.

We simply denote

(2.14)

\widetilde{N}_{a}(w):=\widetilde{N}_{a,2}(w)

when
p=2
in (
2.13
).

We now introduce the parabolic square and area functions.

Definition 2.15
.

For a function
w
with
\nabla w\in L^{2}_{\loc}(\Omega)
, we define the square function of
w
by

(2.16)

S_{a}(w)(q,\tau):=\left(\iint_{\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}\left|\nabla w(X,t)\right|^{2}\delta(X,t)^{-n}dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}\quad\text{for }(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega,

and for
p\in(1,\infty)
, define the
p
-adapted square function by

(2.17)

S_{p,a}(w)(q,\tau):=\left(\iint_{\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}\left|\nabla w(X,t)\right|^{2}|w|^{p-2}\delta(X,t)^{-n}dX\,dt\right)^{1/p}\quad\text{for }(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega.

We also need the following version of the area function defined as in
[
11
]
. For a function
w:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, we define

(2.18)

A_{a}(w)(q,\tau):=\left(\iint_{\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}|\partial_{t}w(X,t)|^{2}\delta(X,t)^{-n+2}\,dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}

If
w
satisfies the parabolic PDE (
1.1
), then heuristically
\partial_{t}w\sim\nabla^{2}w
, and so the operators
A_{a}
and
S_{a}
can be related using Caccioppoli type estimates. In particular, as shown in
[
14
, Section 6]
, for a solution
u
to (
1.1
), one can prove the pointwise bound

(2.19)

A_{a}^{2}(\nabla u)(q,\tau)\lesssim S_{a}^{2}(\nabla u)(q,\tau)+\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(q,\tau)}(|\partial_{t}A|^{2}x_{n}^{2}+|\nabla A|^{2})|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt,

as well as the
L^{p}
estimate for
p>1

(2.20)

\|A_{a}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\leq C\|S_{a}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}+C\|\mu\|_{C}^{1/2}\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}.

We shall derive similar estimates for
H=a_{nj}\partial_{j}u
in Section
4.2
.

It is well-known (using a level-sets argument) that for
p\in(0,\infty)
, the
L^{p}
norms of
N_{a}(w)
,
\widetilde{N}_{a}(w)
,
S_{a}(w)
,
A_{a}(w)
and
\tilde{A}_{a}(w)
are invariant under changes of
a
up to a constant multiple. For this reason, we omit the dependence on the aperture
a
of the cones when there is no need for the specificity.

2.1.
Reinforced weak and Energy solutions

We recall the paper
[
2
]
that neatly presents the concept of reinforced weak solutions for the parabolic problem of interest here. In
[
8
]
, it was shown that the definition given in
[
2
]
can be weakened a bit further by only asking for the “local
1/2
derivative” in the time variable. We explain below.

If
\mathcal{O}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we let
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(\mathcal{O})
be the standard Sobolev space of real valued functions
v
defined on
\mathcal{O}
, such that
v
and
\nabla v
are in
L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R})
and
L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, respectively. A subscripted ‘
\loc
’ will indicate that these conditions hold locally.

We shall say that
u
is a
reinforced weak solution
of
-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
on
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
if

\displaystyle u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{\loc}(\Omega):=\mathcal{H}_{\loc}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R};L^{2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))\cap L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{W}^{1,2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))

and if for all
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

(2.21)

\iint_{\Omega}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla(\phi\psi)}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}(u\psi)\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\phi}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}(u\phi)\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\psi}\right]\,\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Here,
D_{t}^{1/2}
is the half-order derivative and
H_{t}
is the Hilbert transform with respect to the
t
variable, normalized so that
\partial_{t}=D_{t}^{1/2}H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}
. The space
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
is the homogeneous Sobolev space of order 1/2 - the completion of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
in the norm
\|D_{t}^{1/2}(\cdot)\|_{2}
- and it embeds into the space
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{C}
of tempered distributions, modulo constants.
The local space
\mathcal{H}_{\loc}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
consists of functions
u
such that
u\phi\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. As shown in
[
8
]
the space
\mathcal{H}_{\loc}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
is larger than
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
.
For
u\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R};L^{2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O})),
(
2.21
) simplifies to

\iint_{\Omega}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla\phi}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\phi}\right]\,\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Our definition has the advantage that, in taking a cut-off of the function
u
, we might potentially improve the decay of
D^{1/2}_{t}u
at infinity.

At this point we remark that for any
u\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the formula

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}(u\psi)\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\phi}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}(u\phi)\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\psi}\right]\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}u\cdot{\partial_{t}(\phi\psi)}\,\mathrm{d}t

holds, where on the right-hand side we use the duality form extension of the complex inner product of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, between
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
and its dual
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{-1/2}(\mathbb{R})
. By taking
\psi=1
on the set where
\phi
is supported, it follows that a reinforced weak solution is a weak solution in the usual sense on
\Omega
since it satisfies

u\in L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{W}^{1,2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))
and for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla\phi}\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t-\iint_{\Omega}u\cdot{\partial_{t}\phi}\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

This implies
\partial_{t}u\in L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{W}^{-1,2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))
. Conversely, any weak solution
u
in
\mathcal{H}_{\loc}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R};L^{2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))
is a reinforced weak solution.

Specializing to the case
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathcal{O}
is either a bounded or an unbounded Lipschitz domain,
we say that a
reinforced weak solution
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
belongs to the
energy class

\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
if

\displaystyle\|v\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}:=\bigg(\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\|H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}<\infty.

Consequently, these are called
energy solutions
. When considered modulo constants,
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
is a Hilbert space and it is in fact the closure of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}\!\big(\,\overline{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\,\big)
for the homogeneous norm
\|\cdot\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}

and it coincides with the space
\dot{L}^{2}_{1,1/2}(\Omega)
.

As shown in
[
2
]
(with a small generalization), functions from
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
have well-defined Dirichlet traces with values in
the
homogeneous parabolic Sobolev space

\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
. Here,
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{s}_{\pm\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as the closure of Schwartz functions
v\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with Fourier support away from the origin in the norm
\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}((|\xi|^{2}\pm i\tau)^{s}\mathcal{F}v)\|_{2}
. This yields a space of tempered distributions modulo constants in
L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
if
0<s\leq 1/2
.
Conversely, any
g\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}
can be extended to a function
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
with trace
v\big|_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}=g
. For this via partition of unity we can define

\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
for
\mathcal{O}
Lipschitz.

Hence, by the energy solution to
-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
with Dirichlet boundary datum
u\big|_{\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}=f\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}
(understood in the trace sense) we mean
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
such that

\displaystyle a(u,v):=\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla v}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}v}\right]\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t=0,

holds for all
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}
, the subspace of
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
with zero boundary trace.

Moving onto the Neumann problem, given any
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
, the co-normal derivative
\partial^{A}_{\nu}u\Big|_{\partial\Omega}=:\langle A\nabla u,\nu\rangle\Big|_{\partial\Omega}=g
is defined via the formula

(2.22)

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla v}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}v}\right]\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}gv\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=0,

for all
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
. Here, since the traces of
v
belong to
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
and all elements of the space
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}
are realized by some
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
,
the Neumann boundary data must by duality naturally belong to the space
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{-1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
.

By
[
2
]
, the key to solving these problems is the introduction of the modified sesquilinear form (introduced earlier in
[
28
]
):

(2.23)

a_{\delta}(u,v):=\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla(1-\delta H_{t})v}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}(1-\delta H_{t})v}\right]\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
\delta
is a real number yet to be chosen. The Hilbert transform
H_{t}
is a skew-symmetric isometric operator with inverse
-H_{t}
on both
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
and
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}
. Hence,
1-\delta H_{t}
is invertible on these spaces for any
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence for a fixed
\delta>0
small enough,
a_{\delta}
is coercive on
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
since

(2.24)

a_{\delta}(u,u)\geq(\lambda-\Lambda\delta)\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}+\delta\|H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\|_{2}^{2}.

In particular
\delta=\lambda/(\Lambda+1)
would work.
To solve the Dirichlet problem we take an extension
w\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
of the data
f
and apply the Lax-Milgram lemma to
a_{\delta}
on
\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}
to obtain some
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}
such that

\displaystyle a_{\delta}(u,v)=-a_{\delta}(w,v)\qquad(v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}).

Hence,
u+w
is an energy solution with data
f
. Should there exist another solution
v
, then
a_{\delta}(u+w-v,u+w-v)=0
and hence by coercivity
\|u+w-v\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}=0
. Thus the two solutions only differ by a constant. It means that Dirichlet problem associated with our parabolic PDE is
well-posed
in the energy class. Similar arguments allow us to solve the Neumann problem by considering for the datum
g\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{-1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
the solution
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
such that

\displaystyle a_{\delta}(u,v)=\langle g,\mbox{Tr }(1-\delta H_{t})v\rangle\qquad(v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}).

Definition 2.25

(
(D)_{p^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
)
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
. We say that the
L^{p^{\prime}}
Dirichlet problem is solvable for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, denoted by
(D)_{p^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
, if there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
g\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}\cap L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
, the energy solution
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
to
\mathcal{L}^{*}u=\partial_{t}u+\divg(A^{T}\nabla u)=0
in
\Omega
with trace
u|_{\partial\Omega}=g
satisfies the estimate

\|N(u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

Definition 2.26

(
(N)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
)
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
. We say that the
L^{p}
Neumann problem is solvable for
\mathcal{L}
, denoted by
(N)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
, if there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
g\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{-1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)\cap L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
, the energy solution
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
to
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
with
\partial_{\nu}^{A}u|_{\partial\Omega}=g
(defined as in (
2.22
)) satisfies the estimate

\|\widetilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

3.
Proof of Theorem
1.6
for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.6
for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
using results obtained in Sections
5
-
7
. The case where
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
with
\mathcal{O}
being an unbounded Lipschitz graph domain (that is, of the form (
2.3
)) with sufficiently small Lipschitz constant follows from a standard bi-Lipschitz change of variables, and the case where
\mathcal{O}
is a bounded Lipschitz domain with sufficiently small Lipschitz constant is treated in Section
8
.

We first state a perturbation result that is proven in
[
30
]
.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
30
, Theorem 1.6]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\partial_{t}+\divg(A_{0}\nabla\cdot)
,
\mathcal{L}_{1}=-\partial_{t}+\divg(A_{1}\nabla\cdot)
be two parabolic operators on
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
, with
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
Lipschitz. Assume that

(3.2)

d\nu:=\sup_{B_{X,t}}|A_{0}-A_{1}|^{2}\frac{dX\,dt}{\delta(X,t)}\quad\text{ is a Carleson measure on }\Omega.

If
(N)_{q_{0}}^{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
and
(D)_{q_{0}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}_{1}^{*}}
are solvable for some
q_{0}>1
, then there exists a small
\varepsilon>0
depending on
n
, the ellipticity constants of
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
,
\mathcal{O}
and
q_{0}
, such that if
\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}\leq\varepsilon
, then
(N)_{q_{0}}^{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
is solvable.

We need the perturbation result above because we want the coefficients to be regular enough to apply the estimates in Sections
4
-
7
. This can be achieved by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose that the matrix
A
satisfies (
1.2
) and either (
1.3
) or (
1.4
) in
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Then there exist matrices
B
and
D
such that
A=B+D
, where
B
satisfies (
1.2
) with the same constants
\lambda
and
\Lambda
. In addition,
B
has entries in
C^{\infty}(\Omega)
, and the following estimates hold:

(3.4)

d\mu^{\prime}:=\sup_{B_{X,t}}\left(\left|\nabla B\right|^{2}\delta+\left|\partial_{t}B\right|^{2}\delta^{3}+\left|D\right|^{2}\delta^{-1}\right)dX\,dt\quad\text{ is a Carleson measure on }\Omega,

with
\left\|\mu^{\prime}\right\|_{C}\leq C\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}
for some
C>0
, and

(3.5)

\left|x_{n}\nabla B(X,t)\right|+\left|x_{n}^{2}\partial_{t}B(X,t)\right|+\left|x_{n}^{i+2j}\nabla^{i}\partial_{t}^{j}B(X,t)\right|\leq C\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}^{1/2}\quad\text{ for all }(X,t)\in\Omega,

for
i,j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
i+j\geq 1
where the constant
C
depends only on
n
,
\Lambda
(and possibly also
i
and
j
).

A similar version of Lemma
3.3
is proven in
[
14
, Lemma 3.32]
and so we shall not prove Lemma
3.3
in detail. The key differences from
[
14
, Lemma 3.32]
are (1) here we are taking the supremum over parabolic Whitney balls in all the Carleson estimates, and (2) the bound (
3.5
) depends on the Carleson constant
\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}
. One can see from the proof of
[
14
, Lemma 3.32]
that once we assume the Carleson condition with the supremum for
A
, we can achieve (
3.4
) with the supremum. As for (
3.5
), it follows from the fact that

\sup_{B_{X,t}}\left(\left|\nabla B\right|^{2}\delta+\left|\partial_{t}B\right|^{2}\delta^{3}+\left|\nabla^{i}\partial_{t}^{j}B\right|^{2}\delta^{2i+4j-1}\right)\,dX\,dt\quad\text{ is a Carleson measure on }\Omega,

with its Carleson constant bounded above by
C\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}
, which can be proven similarly as in
[
14
, Lemma 3.32]
.

Thanks to Lemma
3.3
and Theorem
3.1
, it suffices to prove Theorem
1.6
for parabolic operators whose coefficient matrix satisfies the condition (
3.5
) instead of just (
1.3
) or (
1.4
).

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
. Choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
4(m-1)<p\leq 4m
and set
\mathfrak{p}=4m
; note that
\mathfrak{p}\geq\max\left\{p,4\right\}
. We will show that

(3.6)

\text{there exists }\delta>0\text{ such that }(N)_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathcal{L}}\text{ is solvable if }\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}<\delta\text{ in }\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}.

Once we obtain this, we choose
\delta
small enough so that
(D)_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
is solvable, which is possible due to
[
9
, Theorem 1.1]
. Then
(N)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
follows from interpolation of the Neumann problem (
[
15
, Theorem 1.6]
), as desired.

To prove (
3.6
), let
g\in L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)\cap\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{-1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
and let
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
be the energy solution to the Neumann problem
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
with
\partial_{\nu}^{A}u|_{\partial\Omega}=g
. We assume a priori that
\left\|\widetilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
. We denote by
H:=a_{nj}\partial_{j}u
(using summation convention) which at the boundary
\partial\Omega
gives us the co-normal derivative of
u
.

We first bound the nontangential maximal function of
\nabla u
by
p
-adapted square functions, which is done in Section
5
. Precisely, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, Theorems
5.65
and
5.84
allow us to find
\delta>0
such that if
\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}<\delta
(recall that we have additionally (
3.5
)), then there holds

(3.7)

\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla_{T}u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\left\|S_{\mathfrak{p}}(\nabla_{T}u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon\left(\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\left\|S_{2}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\right),

and

(3.8)

\|{\tilde{N}}(H)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\left\|S_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon\left(\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\left\|S_{2}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\right).

Next, we control the
p
-adapted square functions
\left\|S_{\mathfrak{p}}(\nabla_{T}u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}
and
\left\|S_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}
in Section
6
. Specifically, the two key steps carried out in the proof of Lemma
6.1
are

\left\|S_{\mathfrak{p}}(\nabla_{T}u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\left\|\nabla_{T}u\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon\left(\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\left\|S_{2}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\right),

and

\left\|\nabla_{T}u\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla_{T}u|^{\mathfrak{p}-2}|\nabla H|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+\varepsilon\left(\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\left\|S_{2}(\nabla_{T}u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\right).

Combining the two estimates with (
3.7
), one obtains the estimate summarized in Lemma
6.1
:

(3.9)

\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla_{T}u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla_{T}u|^{\mathfrak{p}-2}|\nabla H|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+\varepsilon\,C\left(\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\left\|S_{2}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\right).

We need to control the term
\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla_{T}u|^{\mathfrak{p}-2}|\nabla H|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt
and
\left\|S_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}
. Both terms are taken care of by Lemma
6.24
, which shows that for any integer
0\leq k\leq\mathfrak{p}-2
,

(3.10)

\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla_{T}u|^{\mathfrak{p}-k-2}|H|^{k}|\nabla H|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\leq C\int_{\partial\Omega}|H|^{\mathfrak{p}}\,dx\,dt\\
+C(\mathfrak{p}-k-2)\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla_{T}u|^{\mathfrak{p}-k-3}|H|^{k+1}|\nabla H|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+\varepsilon\,C\left[\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\|S(\nabla u)\|^{\mathfrak{p}}_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}\right].

In fact, letting
k=\mathfrak{p}-2
in (
3.10
) gives that

\left\|S_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\left\|H\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon\,C\left[\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\|S(\nabla u)\|^{\mathfrak{p}}_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}\right],

and applying (
3.10
) inductively for
k=0,1,\dots,\mathfrak{p}-2
gives that

\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla_{T}u|^{\mathfrak{p}-2}|\nabla H|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\leq C\left\|H\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon\,C\left[\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\|S(\nabla u)\|^{\mathfrak{p}}_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}\right].

Therefore, by (
3.9
) and (
3.8
), one obtains that

\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla_{T}u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\left\|H\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon\left(\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\left\|S_{2}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\right),

and

\|{\tilde{N}}(H)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\left\|H\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon\left(\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\left\|S_{2}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\right).

Since
\widetilde{N}(\partial_{n}u)
is related to
{\widetilde{N}}(\nabla_{T}u)
and
{\widetilde{N}}(H)
by using the definition of
H
and the bounds for the matrix
A
:

|\widetilde{N}(\partial_{n}u)|\leq C|\widetilde{N}(H)|+C|\widetilde{N}(\nabla_{T}u)|,

we have that

\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\leq C\left\|H\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\varepsilon\left(\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}+\left\|S_{2}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}^{\mathfrak{p}}\right).

This is almost what we want, except that we still have
\left\|S_{2}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}
on the right-hand side. This is not an issue, as by Theorem
7.1
,
\left\|S(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}}\leq C\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}}\leq C\|\widetilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}}
. Now we choose

\varepsilon\in(0,1)
sufficiently small, so that we get that

(3.11)

\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\left\|H\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}=C\left\|g\right\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)},

which proves (
3.6
) under the a priori assumption
\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
.

It remains to justify the finiteness of
\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}
. We split the argument into multiple steps, some of which are similar to those of
[
14
]
, while others are new.
The arguments required to justify this a priori finiteness, even assuming smooth coefficients and smooth Neumann data, may seem surprisingly elaborate. In fact, they do not appear in the current literature, even for smooth coefficients, in the context of an unbounded domain. The steps below involve the following key components: (1) reducing to operators with smooth coefficients whose gradient is finitely supported, and to smooth data; (2) using the inhomogeneous PDE satisfied by the tangential derivatives of the solution (
\partial_{i}u,\,i<n
), but complicated by the fact that the right-hand side involves all derivatives; (3) using the inhomogeneous PDE satisfied by the conormal derivative (
\sum_{j}a_{n,j}\partial_{j}
) to get the missing estimates on
\partial_{n}u
; (4) deriving a Moser iteration argument to improve regularity of
|\nabla u|
that requires a careful intertwining of the estimates obtained on the tangential derivatives and the conormal derivative. The essential bootstrap platform is provided in Lemma
3.16
.

Step 1. Approximation.
We approximate a matrix
B
as in (
3.5
) by a sequence of matrices
B^{i}
,
i=1,2,3,\dots
as follows.

Let
\eta^{i}
be a smooth non-negative cutoff function in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
that is equal to
1
on a parabolic ball
B(0,i)
, vanishing outside the ball
B(0,2i)
. Consider

(3.12)

B^{i}(x,x_{n},t)=\eta^{i}(x,x_{n},t)B(x,x_{n}+1/i,t)+(1-\eta^{i})I,

where
I
is the
n\times n
identity matrix. All matrices
B^{i}
on the domain
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
are uniformly elliptic, with the same ellipticity constants, and each
B^{i}
is the identity matrix outside a large ball centered at zero. Moreover,

\|\nabla^{j}\partial^{k}_{t}B^{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}<\infty
for all nonnegative integers
j,k
such that
j+k\geq 1
.
This is a consequence of the fact that
|\nabla B|\leq Cx_{n}^{-1}
and thus
|\nabla B^{i}|\leq C(x_{n}+1/i)^{-1}\leq Ci
, with similar arguments for
\partial_{t}B^{i}
and all higher derivatives.
In addition, each
B^{i}
satisfies the same Carleson measure conditions as
B
does and thus
B^{i}
satisfies
(
3.4
) with the same constants that hold for
B
. Finally, we observe that
B^{i}\to B
uniformly in the
L^{\infty}
norm as
i\to\infty
on all compact subsets
K\subset\Omega
and
B^{i}\to B
in
L^{p}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(0,0))
, for
p<\infty
for any
R>0
.

Consider the approximate energy solutions
u^{i}
to the PDE problems

\mathcal{L}^{i}u^{i}=\divg(B^{i}\nabla u^{i})-\partial_{t}u^{i}=0\quad\mbox{in }\Omega\quad\mbox{with}\quad\partial^{B^{i}}_{\nu}u^{i}\Big|_{\partial\Omega}=g,

where
g
is in the trace space

\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{-1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
. Since, for all
i
, the ellipticity constants have the same bounds, the energy norms
\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
of the
u^{i}
are uniformly bounded by a constant that only depends on the
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{-1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
norm of
g
.

The solutions
u^{i}
are actually determined up to a constant, and enjoy interior Hölder continuity, so it may be assumed without loss of generality that

u^{i}(0,1,0)=0
for all
i
. With this in hand, and the fact that
\sup_{i}\|u^{i}\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)}<\infty

we conclude that there exists a subsequence
(u^{i_{n}})
of the sequence
(u^{i})

converging weakly in
\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
to a function
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
with

u(0,1,0)=0
. From this point, the argument is analogous to that of Section 4 (Step 5) of
[
14
]
and hence we omit the details. In summary, there exists a subsequence
u^{i_{n}}
converging strongly on compact subsets of
\Omega
.

In step 5, we will prove that the approximate solutions
u^{i}
have the a priori bound
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u^{i})\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
(as required above for
g
with finite
{L}^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)
norm).
Assuming this bound for the moment, by (
3.11
) we have that for each
i
,

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u^{i})\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|g\|_{{L}^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)},

with a constant
C
which is independent of
i
, depending only on ellipticity of the original matrix
B
and the Carleson norm of its coefficients. As in
[
14
]
we then conclude that

(3.13)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|g\|_{{L}^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}.

To see that
u
attains the boundary value
g
, observe that for each
i
and
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
by
(
2.22
)

\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\left[B^{i}\nabla u^{i}\cdot{\nabla v}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u^{i}\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}v}\right]\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}gv\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Here it suffices to consider Lipschitz test functions
v
with bounded support as they are dense in
\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
. Recall that we have the weak convergence of
u^{i_{n}}\to u
in
\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
. Furthermore, clearly
(B^{i})^{T}\nabla v\to B^{T}\nabla v
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
since
B^{i}\to B
in
L^{p}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(0,0))
for any
p<\infty
and
R>0
and
\nabla v\in L^{\infty}
and has bounded support. Thus we
conclude that

\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\left[\nabla u\cdot{B^{T}\nabla v}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}v}\right]\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}gv\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Hence
u
is the energy solution of the parabolic PDE with matrix
B
with Neumann boundary datum
g
as desired.

Step 2. Reduction to an inhomogeneous problem.
It follows that we only need to prove that
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u^{i})\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty

for solutions
u^{i}
as defined above, associated to matrices
B^{i}

having the properties listed in step 1. We fix
i\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a matrix
B^{i}
as above. Dropping the index
i
we may assume that
B
is uniformly elliptic, satisfies the Carleson measure condition (
3.4
), equals the identity matrix outside a large ball centered at zero, and also has the property
that
\|\nabla^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}_{t}B\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}<\infty
for all nonnegative integers
\alpha,\beta
such that
\alpha+\beta\geq 1
.
Let
u
be an energy solution to
\mathcal{L}u=0
where
\mathcal{L}=\divg(B\nabla\cdot)-\partial_{t}
. Without loss of generality assume also that
\partial_{\nu}^{B}u\Big|_{\partial\Omega}=g
for some smooth function
g
(in all variables).
It is sufficient to establish solvability for a class of functions that is dense in our underlying space since it then extends continuously to the whole space, thanks to estimate (
3.13
).

We aim to find a differentiable function
v
supported in a bounded set
\Omega\cap B_{2R}(0,0)
, for some large
R>0
, satisfying
\partial_{\nu}^{B}v=g
on
\partial\Omega
. We start by choosing
R>0
large enough such that outside the ball
B_{R}(0,0)
we have
g=0
on
\partial\Omega

and
B=I
. We now find a smooth domain
\tilde{\Omega}
such that

\Omega\cap B_{R}(0,0)\subset\tilde{\Omega}\subset\Omega\cap B_{2R}(0,0).

With variable
\partial_{t}
temporarily thought of as
\partial_{0}
we envision

\tilde{\Omega}
as a smooth bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with variables
x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
.
We introduce a new
(n+1)\times(n+1)
matrix
\tilde{B}
defined by

\tilde{B}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}1&0\\
\hline\cr 0&B\\
\end{array}\right].

Let
\tilde{g}
be a smooth function on
\partial\tilde{\Omega}
such that
\tilde{g}=g
on
\partial\tilde{\Omega}\cap\partial\Omega
, but also satisfying the condition
\int_{\partial\tilde{\Omega}}\tilde{g}=0
.
For this data
\tilde{g}
, one can solve the elliptic Neumann problem:

\divg_{x_{0},x}(\tilde{B}\nabla_{x_{0},x}\tilde{v})=0\quad\mbox{in }\tilde{\Omega},\qquad\partial_{\nu}^{\tilde{B}}\tilde{v}=\tilde{g}\quad\mbox{on }\partial\tilde{\Omega},

by the method of layer potentials.
(For example, see
[
7
]
Theorem 4.4 for solvability via the method of layer potentials.)
In short, we have “ellipticized” the problem.
The new matrix
\tilde{B}
has the required smoothness - it is twice differentiable in
x_{0}
and
x
. It follows that

\nabla_{x,x_{0}}\tilde{v}\in C^{\alpha}(\tilde{\Omega})
for some
\alpha>0
; and in fact, as the matrix
\tilde{B}

is smooth,
\tilde{v}
has as high differentiability as one might require. Observe that on

\partial\tilde{\Omega}\cap\partial\Omega
, it is the case that
\partial_{\nu}^{\tilde{B}}\tilde{v}=\partial_{\nu}^{B}\tilde{v}
and hence

\tilde{v}
satisfies our original Neumann data. The final step is to multiply
\tilde{v}
by a smooth cutoff function
\varphi

supported on
B_{2R}(0,0)
, equal to
1
near the set
\partial\Omega\cap B_{R}(0,0)
which is constant in the
x_{n}
variable near the set
\partial\Omega
. All of this is achievable and the resulting function
v=\tilde{v}\varphi

is supported in a bounded set
\Omega\cap B_{2R}(0,0)
for some large
R>0
and
\partial_{\nu}^{B}v=g
on
\partial\Omega
. It now follows that
u=v+w
where
w\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
solves the parabolic PDE:

-\partial_{t}w+\divg(B\nabla w):=h=-\partial_{t}v+\divg(B\nabla v)\quad\mbox{in }\Omega,\qquad\partial_{\nu}^{B}w=0\quad\mbox{on }\partial\Omega.

Here
h
on the right-hand side is an
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
function with bounded support.
If we prove that
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla w)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
we are done, since
\nabla v\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)

has bounded support, and hence
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
for any
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

Let us now use an even reflection and extend the function
w
across the boundary
x_{n}=0
. The extended solution
\tilde{w}
is defined as

\tilde{w}(X,t)=\begin{cases}w(x,x_{n},t),&\quad\mbox{when }x_{n}>0,\\
w(x,-x_{n},t),&\quad\mbox{when }x_{n}<0,\end{cases}

and solves the parabolic PDE:

(3.14)

-\partial_{t}\tilde{w}+\divg(\tilde{B}\nabla\tilde{w}):=\tilde{h}

in the whole domain

\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. Here
\tilde{h}
is the even reflection of
h
, and the coefficients of the matrix
\tilde{B}

are bounded functions of all variables and in fact smooth in
x
and
x_{n}
, except on the line
x_{n}=0
. Obviously,
\tilde{B}
is uniformly elliptic. By interior Hölder regularity of solutions it follows that
\tilde{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
.

Step 3. Regularity of
\nabla\tilde{w}
.
Let us first consider
\partial_{i}\tilde{w}
, for
i=1,2\dots,n-1
.
For simplicity of notation set
w_{i}=\partial_{i}\tilde{w}
. Clearly,
\tilde{B}
is differentiable with respect to
x_{i}

as the only discontinuity of
\tilde{B}
is at
x_{n}=0
in the direction parallel to the vector
e_{i}
. Hence
w_{i}
satisfies the PDE

(3.15)

-\partial_{t}w_{i}+\divg(\tilde{B}\nabla w_{i}):=\partial_{i}\tilde{h}-\divg((\partial_{i}\tilde{B})\nabla\tilde{w})

in the whole domain
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. A priori we know that
\partial_{i}\tilde{B}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
and
\nabla\tilde{w}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
because
w\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
.
Since it is also the case that
\tilde{h}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
, this implies that
w_{i}\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
,
i=1,2,\dots,n-1
.

We can say more about the regularity of
w_{i}
, namely that
w_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and also
w_{i}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, as the following lemma asserts.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
2\leq p<\infty
. Suppose
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
is an energy solution of the PDE

-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=-h+\divg F,\qquad\quad\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},

where
A
is elliptic, measurable and bounded and

(3.17)

S:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h|^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}dt+\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}dt\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

Assume that solution
u
has the normalization:
u(\cdot,t)\to 0
as
t\to-\infty
.

Then
u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R},L^{np/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and

(3.18)

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u|^{np/(n-2)}dx\right)^{(n-2)/n}dt\right)^{1/p}\lesssim S.

Proof.

We split the solution into two parts,
u=u_{1}+u_{2}
,
where
u_{1}
solves the PDE with the right-hand side
-\divg F
, and
u_{2}
has
h
on its right-hand side.
First, we consider
u_{1}
. It follows that after we multiply the PDE
u_{1}
satisfies by
|u_{1}|^{p-2}u_{1}
and integrate over the spatial variables we get that

(3.19)

p^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{1}|^{p}dx=-(p-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}A\nabla u_{1}\cdot\nabla u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p-2}\,dx\\
+(p-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F\cdot\nabla u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p-2}dx.

For the first term on the right-hand side we have (by ellipticity)

-(p-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}A\nabla u_{1}\cdot\nabla u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p-2}\,dx\leq-\frac{4(p-1)}{p^{2}}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p/2-1})|^{2}dx,

which is strictly negative. Using Cauchy-Schwarz for the last term of (
3.19
), and then Hölder’s inequality, we get

(3.20)

\left|(p-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F\cdot\nabla u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p-2}dx\right|\leq\frac{2(p-1)}{p^{2}}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p/2-1})|^{2}dx\\
+\frac{p-1}{2}\lambda^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{2}|u_{1}|^{p-2}dx\leq\frac{2(p-1)}{p^{2}}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p/2-1})|^{2}dx\\
+\frac{p-1}{2}\lambda^{-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{1}|^{p}dx\right)^{(p-2)/p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}.

From (
3.19
), moving the negative term to the left-hand side, we see
that

(3.21)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{1}|^{p}dx+\frac{2(p-1)}{p}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p/2-1})|^{2}dx\\
\leq\frac{(p-1)p}{2}\lambda^{-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{1}|^{p}dx\right)^{(p-2)/p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}.

Dropping the (positive) second term in the inequality and rearranging the remaining terms gives

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{1}|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}\leq(p-1)\lambda^{-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}.

Integrating over the interval
(-\infty,t]
, and recalling that

u_{1}\to 0
as
t\to-\infty
,
yields

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{1}(t,x)|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}\leq(p-1)\lambda^{-1}\int_{-\infty}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F(\tau,x)|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}d\tau.

It follows that

(3.22)

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|u_{1}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq(p-1)^{1/2}\lambda^{-1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}dt\right)^{1/2}.

Inserting this estimate for the “
u_{1}
” integrand on the right-hand side of (
3.21
) gives

(3.23)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{1}|^{p}dx+\frac{2(p-1)}{p}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p/2-1})|^{2}dx\\
\leq\frac{[(p-1)\lambda^{-1}]^{p/2}p}{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F(\tau,x)|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}d\tau\right)^{(p-2)/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}.

After integrating (
3.23
) over
\mathbb{R}
, we conclude that

(3.24)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla(u_{1}|u_{1}|^{p/2-1})|^{2}dxd\tau\\
\leq\frac{(p-1)^{p/2-1}p^{2}\lambda^{-p/2-1}}{4}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F(\tau,x)|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}d\tau\right)^{(p-2)/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}d\tau\\
=\frac{(p-1)^{p/2-1}p^{2}\lambda^{-p/2-1}}{4}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F(\tau,x)|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}d\tau\right)^{p/2}.

We see that

u_{1}(t,\cdot)|u_{1}(t,\cdot)|^{p/2-1}\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and therefore
u_{1}(t,\cdot)|u_{1}(t,\cdot)|^{p/2-1}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
q=2n/(n-2)
.
By Sobolev embedding in the spatial variables and (
3.24
) we get that

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{1}|^{np/(n-2)}dx\right)^{(n-2)/n}dt\right)^{1/p}\leq C(p,\lambda)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|F|^{p}dx\right)^{2/p}dt\right)^{1/2},

or in other words,

\|u_{1}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R},L^{np/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq C(p,\lambda)\|F\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}.

The calculation for
u_{2}
is analogous. We start by observing that for the term containing
h
, by Hölder’s inequality,

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}hu_{2}|u_{2}|^{p-2}dx\right|\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{2}|^{p}dx\right)^{(p-1)/p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h|^{p}dx\right)^{1/p},

which, as above, then yields the estimate

(3.25)

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|u_{2}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h|^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}dt.

Continuing the calculation as in (
3.23
) we also find that

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u_{2}|^{np/(n-2)}dx\right)^{(n-2)/n}dt\right)^{1/p}\leq C(p,\lambda)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h|^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}dt,

or in other words,

\|u_{2}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R},L^{np/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq C(p,\lambda)\|h\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R},L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

We can apply the lemma to each
w_{i}
to see that
w_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R},L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
as desired.

Step 4. Regularity of
H
.
In step 3 we obtained an initial estimate for
\nabla_{x}\tilde{w}
, and hence for
\nabla_{x}u
. It remains to obtain a good estimate for
\partial_{n}u
. To this end, we use the
results of subsection
4.2
, namely that

\partial_{n}u=a_{nn}^{-1}(H-\sum_{j<n}a_{nj}\partial_{j}u).

We have shown that
a_{nn}^{-1}\sum_{j<n}a_{nj}\partial_{j}u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, and we now aim to establish the same result for
H
, and thus also for
\partial_{n}u
.
Recall that
H
solves the PDE given by (
4.2
) in
\Omega
with left-hand side given by (
4.3
).
At the boundary,
H
is equal to
g
, and so
H
solves a Dirichlet problem. We decompose
H
as
H_{0}+H_{1}
, where
-\partial_{t}H_{0}+\tilde{L}H_{0}=0
with Dirichlet datum
g
, while

H_{1}
solves
-\partial_{t}H_{1}+\tilde{L}H_{1}=F
with vanishing Dirichlet boundary condition. The results for solvability of the
L^{p}
Dirichlet problem (
[
9
]
) imply that
\|\tilde{N}(H_{0})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
, for all
1<p\leq\infty
. It remains to consider
H_{1}
. We use an odd reflection to extend this function
to the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. The right-hand side
F
will be also extended by an odd reflection to a function
\tilde{F}
. Since
H_{1}\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
, the extended function
\tilde{H}_{1}
satisfies

(3.26)

-\partial_{t}\tilde{H}_{1}+\divg((\tilde{B})^{T}\nabla\tilde{H}_{1}):=\tilde{F}

in the whole domain

\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. We now examine
F
, as defined by (
4.2
). Replacing
u_{t}
by
Lu
(justified by using the PDE for
u
), we can write
F
in the form

F=\divg(\varphi(B,\nabla B)\nabla u)+\psi(\partial_{t}B,\nabla^{2}B,\nabla B)\nabla u.

where
\varphi,\psi
are smooth functions enjoying the bounds

(3.27)

|\varphi(B,\nabla B)|\leq C|\nabla B|\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,|\psi(\partial_{t}B,\nabla^{2}B,\nabla B)|\leq C(|\partial_{t}B|+|\nabla^{2}B|+|\nabla B|^{2}).

In particular, after reflection,
\tilde{F}
has the same form as in the right-hand side of Lemma
3.16
,
with (
3.27
) guaranteeing that
\tilde{F}
is compactly supported (
\tilde{B}=I
outside the ball
B_{R}(0,0)
). Because
\nabla u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, Lemma
3.16
implies that

\tilde{H}_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R},L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, and therefore
\partial_{n}u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathcal{O}))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R},L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathcal{O}))
.

Step 5. The bootstrap argument.
We are now in position to apply a Moser iteration argument to increase the regularity of
\nabla u
. Combining steps 3 and 4, we have shown that
\nabla u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathcal{O}))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R},L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathcal{O}))
. Going back to (
3.15
), we observe that one of the terms on the right-hand side,
\partial_{i}\tilde{h}
, has the
correct estimates since
\tilde{h}
is bounded and has bounded support. In addition,
\partial_{i}\tilde{B}
is bounded and also has bounded support. It then follows that

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|(\partial_{i}\tilde{B})\nabla\tilde{w}\right|^{2n/(n-2)}dx\right)^{(n-2)/n}dt\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

Thus the Lemma
3.16
again applies and yields that
w_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
w_{i}\in L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R},L^{2(n/(n-2))^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. A similar argument gives the same conclusion for
\tilde{H}_{1}
, and hence for
\partial_{n}u
. Therefore, we have
\nabla u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathcal{O}))\cap L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathbb{R},L^{2(n/(n-2))^{2}}(\mathcal{O}))
. Iterating this argument at each step improves the regularity of
\nabla u
and ultimately yields, after a finite number of iterations, that
\nabla u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{p}(\mathcal{O}))
for some
p>n
. This implies that the term on the right-hand side of PDE for
w_{i}
is of the form
\divg F_{i}
for
F_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
while the PDE for
\tilde{H}_{1}
had right-hand side of the form
h_{n}+\divg F_{n}
for
h_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{p/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
,
F_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. Now a standard parabolic Moser iteration argument yields that
w_{i},\tilde{H}_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
and therefore also

\nabla u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. In particular, this conclusion implies that

\tilde{N}(\nabla u)
is
L^{p}
integrable on bounded subsets of
\partial\Omega
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

For the estimate outside a bounded set, recall that there is an

R
large enough such that the matrix
B
equals
I
in the complement of the ball
B(0,R)
. It follows that, in
\Omega\setminus B(0,R)
, the PDE for
w_{i}
is exactly the heat equation

\Delta w_{i}-\partial_{t}w_{i}=0
.
Hence on this set
w_{i}
is comparable with the fundamental solution for the heat equation. Choosing a pole
(Y,s)
with time
s

in the past of the ball
B(0,R)
, there exists a large constant
M>0
for which
0\leq|w_{i}|\leq MG

on
\partial B(0,R)
(since
w_{i}
is bounded on

\partial B(0,R)
). Hence by the comparison principle, applied to both the positive and negative parts of
w_{i}
,

0<|w_{i}|(x,x_{n},t)\leq MG((x,x_{n},t),(Y,s)),\qquad\mbox{for all }(x,x_{n},t)\notin B(0,R),

where
G
is the Green’s function of the whole space satisfying the bounds

(3.28)

G(X,t,Y,s)\leq C(t-s)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{\left|X-Y\right|^{2}}{C(t-s)}\right)\quad\text{ for all }t>s,

as follows from
[
1
]
.
Note that
G
has sufficient decay - exponential in spatial variables, and polynomial in
t
of order at least
C(2R+t)^{-1}
and is
L^{p}
integrable for
p>1
on an interval
(-R,\infty)
. It follows that

\tilde{N}(\partial_{i}u)
,
i<n
will be integrable in
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
for all
p>1
. Again, a similar argument applies to

\tilde{N}(H_{1})
and hence
\tilde{N}(\partial_{n}u)
.
This establishes the required a priori bound
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{p}}}<\infty
.

4.
Caccioppoli-type inequalities

4.1.
Caccioppoli with cut-off for
\nabla\partial_{t}u

Lemma 4.1

(Caccioppoli with cut-off)
.

Let
u
be a weak solution to
-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
in
\Omega
. Let
\Psi
be a cutoff function that satisfies
0\leq\Psi\leq 1
,
|\nabla\Psi(X,t)|+x_{n}|\partial_{t}\Psi(X,t)|\lesssim x_{n}^{-1}
for
(X,t)\in\Omega
. Let
Q
be a parabolic Whitney ball in
\Omega
with size
r
,
2Q\subset\Omega
. Then for any
m\geq 2
,

\iint_{Q}|\nabla\partial_{t}u|^{2}\Psi^{m}dX\,dt\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{2Q}|\partial_{t}u|^{2}\Psi^{m-2}dX\,dt+C\iint_{2Q}|\partial_{t}A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}\Psi^{m}dX\,dt.

Proof.

The proof is a simple modification of the argument in the beginning of
[
14
, Section 6]
. Let
\eta
be a smooth cutoff function with
\supp\eta\subset 2Q
,
\eta=1
on
Q
,
|\nabla\eta|\lesssim r^{-1}
, and
|\partial_{t}\eta|\lesssim r^{-2}
. Since
w=\partial_{t}u
satisfies

-\partial_{t}w+\divg(A\nabla w)=-\divg((\partial_{t}A)\nabla u),

we have

\iint A\nabla w\cdot\nabla w\Psi^{m}\eta^{2}dX\,dt=\underbrace{-\iint(\partial_{t}w)w\Psi^{m}\eta^{2}dX\,dt}_{=:I_{1}}+\underbrace{\iint\divg((\partial_{t}A)\nabla u)w\Psi^{m}\eta^{2}dX\,dt}_{:=I_{2}}\\
-m\iint A\nabla w\nabla\Psi\left(w\Psi^{m-1}\eta^{2}\right)dX\,dt-2\iint A\nabla w\nabla\eta\left(w\Psi^{m}\eta\right)dX\,dt.

For the two unlabeled terms on the right-hand side, we use the bounds
|\nabla\eta|\lesssim r^{-1}
and
|\nabla\Psi|\lesssim x_{n}^{-1}\approx r^{-1}
on
2Q
and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to get that they are bounded by

\varepsilon\iint|\nabla w|^{2}\Psi^{m}\eta^{2}dX\,dt+\frac{C_{\varepsilon}}{r^{2}}\iint_{2Q}w^{2}\Psi^{m-2}dX\,dt.

We integrate by parts in
t
in
I_{1}
to get that

|I_{1}|=\left|m\iint w^{2}\Psi^{m-1}\eta^{2}\partial_{t}\Psi dX\,dt+2\iint w^{2}\Psi^{m}\eta\partial_{t}\eta dX\,dt\right|\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{2Q}w^{2}\Psi^{m-1}dX\,dt

by the bounds on
|\partial_{t}\Psi|
and
|\partial_{t}\eta|
. For
I_{2}
, the divergence theorem gives that

I_{2}=-\iint(\partial_{t}A)\nabla u\nabla w\left(\Psi^{m}\eta^{2}\right)dX\,dt-m\iint(\partial_{t}A)\nabla u\nabla\Psi\left(u\Psi^{m-1}\eta^{2}\right)dX\,dt\\
-2\iint(\partial_{t}A)\nabla u\nabla\eta\left(w\Psi^{m}\eta\right)dX\,dt.

Using the bounds on
|\nabla\Psi|
and
|\nabla\eta|
in the last two terms and Cauchy-Schwarz, one sees that

|I_{2}|\leq\varepsilon\iint|\nabla w|^{2}\Psi^{m}\eta^{2}+(C+C_{\varepsilon})\iint_{2Q}|\partial_{t}A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}\Psi^{m}+\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{2Q}w^{2}\Psi^{m-2}.

The lemma follows from taking
\varepsilon
small in the above estimates.
∎

4.2.
The PDEs for
H
and the Caccioppoli inequalities for
\partial_{t}H
and
\nabla^{2}H

Let
u
be a weak solution to
-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
in
\Omega
. Recall that
H=a_{nj}\partial_{j}u
. We start by deriving PDEs that
H
satisfies.

For an arbitrary
b_{ij}
satisfying the ellipticity condition and sufficient smoothness let us denote by
\tilde{L}
the operator
\partial_{i}(b_{ij}\partial_{j}\cdot)
. Then clearly

\tilde{L}H=\partial_{i}(b_{ij}\partial_{j}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u))=\sum_{j<n}\partial_{i}(b_{ij}\partial_{j}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u))+\partial_{i}(b_{in}\partial_{n}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u)).

Using the PDE that
u
satisfies, we replace
\partial_{n}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u)
by the remaining terms of PDE for
u

to obtain

\tilde{L}H=\sum_{j<n}\left[\partial_{i}(b_{ij}\partial_{j}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u))-\partial_{i}(b_{in}\partial_{j}(a_{jk}\partial_{k}u))\right]+\partial_{i}(b_{in}\partial_{t}u).

Observe that the choice of coefficients
b_{ij}=a_{ji}
implies cancellation of all terms containing three spatial gradients
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}u
. With these coefficients fixed we further observe that

\partial_{i}(b_{in}\partial_{t}u)=\partial_{t}(a_{ni}\partial_{i}u)+(\partial_{i}a_{ni})\partial_{t}u-(\partial_{t}a_{ni})\partial_{i}u=\partial_{t}H+(\partial_{i}a_{ni})\partial_{t}u-(\partial_{t}a_{ni})\partial_{i}u.

Hence it follows that

(4.2)

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}H=-\partial_{t}H+\tilde{L}H=F:=(\partial_{i}a_{ni})\partial_{t}u-(\partial_{t}a_{ni})\partial_{i}u+\sum_{j<n}\left[\partial_{i}(b_{ij}\partial_{j}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u))-\partial_{i}(b_{in}\partial_{j}(a_{jk}\partial_{k}u))\right].

Exploring the nature of the function on the right-hand side of (
4.2
) we see that this function enjoys a bound by

(4.3)

|F|\lesssim|\nabla A||\partial_{t}u|+|\partial_{t}A||\nabla u|+|\nabla A|^{2}|\nabla u|+|\nabla A||\nabla^{2}u|+|\nabla^{2}A||\nabla u|,

since the terms containing
\nabla^{3}u
cancel out.

Alternatively, for certain arguments we will need
b_{nn}=1
. Hence we choose
b_{ij}=a_{ji}/a_{nn}
. This again guarantees that terms where three derivatives
fall on
u
vanish as these are the terms:

(4.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{j<n}[b_{ij}a_{nk}(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}u)-b_{kn}a_{ji}(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}u)]=\sum_{j<n}a_{nn}^{-1}(a_{ji}a_{nk}-a_{nk}a_{ji})\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}u=0.

We also rearrange the term containing
\partial_{t}
derivative and write it as

\partial_{i}(b_{in}\partial_{t}u)=\partial_{t}\left(\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}\partial_{i}u\right)+(\partial_{i}b_{in})\partial_{t}u-(\partial_{t}b_{in})\partial_{i}u=\partial_{t}\left(\frac{H}{a_{nn}}\right)+(\partial_{i}b_{in})\partial_{t}u-(\partial_{t}b_{in})\partial_{i}u.

It follows that

(4.5)

-a_{nn}^{-1}(\partial_{t}H)+\widetilde{L}H=T:=(\partial_{i}b_{in})\partial_{t}u+(\partial_{t}a_{nn}^{-1})H-(\partial_{t}b_{in})\partial_{i}u\\
+\sum_{j<n}[\partial_{i}(b_{ij}\partial_{j}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u))-\partial_{k}(b_{kn}\partial_{j}(a_{ji}\partial_{i}u))].

We will use this PDE for
{H}
in several places.
When our method relies on the assumption that
k<n-1
in the proofs of Subsection
5.2
, we need to use the equation (
4.5
) to get the results for
H
. Otherwise, it is slightly easier to use the equation (
4.2
) for
H
.

Our next aim is to establish a Caccioppoli inequality for
\nabla^{2}H
and we use (
4.2
) to achieve it. In order to achieve this, let
H_{k}=\partial_{k}H
for
k=1,2,\dots,n
. Differentiating through (
4.2
) we obtain

(4.6)

-\partial_{t}H_{k}+\divg(A^{T}(\nabla H_{k}))=\divg_{x}((\partial_{k}A^{T})\nabla H)+\partial_{k}F.

Given an interior parabolic Whitney cube
Q
of side-length
r
with
3Q\subset\Omega

we multiply both sides by
H_{k}\zeta^{2}

where
\zeta
is a cutoff function equal to one on
Q
for an interior Whitney cube and zero outside
2Q
. After integration by parts we obtain:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\iint_{2Q}\left[(H_{k}\zeta)^{2}\right]_{t}\,dX\,dt+\iint_{2Q}A^{T}\nabla(H_{k}\zeta)\cdot\nabla(H_{k}\zeta)\,dX\,dt\\
&=\iint_{2Q}H_{k}^{2}\zeta\zeta_{t}\,dX\,dt+\iint_{2Q}H_{k}^{2}A^{T}\nabla\zeta\cdot\nabla\zeta\,dX\,dt+\iint_{2Q}A\nabla(H_{k}\zeta)\cdot H_{k}\nabla\zeta\,dX\,dt\\
&\quad-\iint_{2Q}A^{T}\nabla(H_{k}\zeta)\cdot H_{k}\nabla\zeta\,dX\,dt-\iint_{2Q}(\partial_{k}A^{T})\nabla H\zeta\cdot(\nabla H_{k}\zeta)\,dX\,dt\\
&\quad-\iint_{2Q}(\partial_{k}A^{T})\nabla H(H_{k}\cdot(\zeta\nabla\zeta))\,dX\,dt-\iint_{2Q}F\partial_{k}(H_{k}\zeta)\zeta\,dX\,dt-\iint_{2Q}FH_{k}\zeta(\partial_{k}\zeta)\,dX\,dt\end{split}

Using the ellipticity and boundedness of the coefficients and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
r|\nabla A|\leq C
, it follows that

\begin{split}&\lambda\iint_{Q}\left|\nabla H_{k}\right|^{2}\,dX\,dt\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{2Q}|\nabla H|^{2}\,dX\,dt+C\iint_{2Q}|F|^{2}\,dX\,dt.\end{split}

for some constant
C=C(\Lambda)
. As this holds for every
k=1,2,\dots,n
it follows that

\begin{split}&\iint_{Q}\left|\nabla^{2}H\right|^{2}\,dX\,dt\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{2Q}|\nabla H|^{2}\,dX\,dt+C\iint_{2Q}|F|^{2}\,dX\,dt.\end{split}

Observe that given the PDE
\partial_{t}H
satisfies we have that
|\partial_{t}H|\lesssim|\nabla^{2}H|+|\nabla A||\nabla H|+|F|
and hence also

(4.7)

\iint_{Q}\left|\partial_{t}H\right|^{2}\,dX\,dt+\iint_{Q}\left|\nabla^{2}H\right|^{2}\,dX\,dt\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{2}}\iint_{2Q}|\nabla H|^{2}\,dX\,dt+\iint_{2Q}|F|^{2}\,dX\,dt.

This is the final version of the Caccioppoli inequality for
H
. We use it to obtain bounds for the Area function of
H
defined earlier. For a fixed
P\in\partial\Omega

we cover
\Gamma_{a}(P)
by a collection of Whitney cubes
(Q_{i})_{i}
of side length
r_{i}
such that
the collection
(2Q_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
has finite overlap. We then
multiply above inequality by
r_{i}^{-n+2}
, and sum over all indices
i
for
Q=Q_{i}
to obtain:

(4.8)

{A}^{2}(H)(P)=\iint_{\Gamma_{a}(P)}|\partial_{t}H|^{2}x_{n}^{-n+2}\,dX\,dt\lesssim\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}|\nabla H|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt

+\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}|F|^{2}x_{n}^{-n+2}\,dX\,dt.

Here
\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)
is a cone of wider aperture (smaller slope) than the original cone so that
\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}\subset\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)
. We note that (
4.8
) also holds if the cones are truncated.
Clearly, the last term of (
4.8
) requires further work using (
4.3
). Given that
|u_{t}|\lesssim|\nabla^{2}u|+|\nabla A||\nabla u|
we obtain using (
4.3
) that

(4.9)

{A}^{2}(H)(P)\lesssim S^{2}(H)(P)+\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}(|\partial_{t}A|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}A|^{2}+|\nabla A|^{4})|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}^{-n+2}\,dX\,dt+

+\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}|\nabla A|^{2}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}x_{n}^{-n+2}\,dX\,dt.

Again, a truncated version of this inequality also holds. Using truncated cones at the height
r
and integrating over a boundary ball
\Delta_{r}
we obtain that (after using
|\nabla A|x_{n}\leq C
in one of the terms):

\|A(H)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Delta_{r})}\lesssim\|S(H)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(2\Delta_{r})}+\iint_{T(2\Delta_{r})}(|\partial_{t}A|^{2}x_{n}^{2}+|\nabla^{2}A|^{2}x_{n}^{2}+|\nabla A|^{2})|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt

+\iint_{T(2\Delta_{r})}|\nabla A|^{2}|x_{n}\nabla^{2}u|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\leq\|S(H)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(2\Delta_{r})}+\|\mu\|_{C}\|N(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(2\Delta_{r})},

using the Carleson condition for
\mu
for the operator
A
. We are also using the fact that

N(x_{n}\nabla^{2}u)\lesssim N(\nabla u)
using the Caccioppoli inequality for the second gradient of
u
.
In particular after taking
r\to\infty
we obtain

(4.10)

\|A(H)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\leq C\|S(H)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}+C\|\mu\|_{C}^{1/2}\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}.

We remark that the
L^{p}
version of this result also holds; we refer the reader to
[
14
, Section 10]
and in particular the equation (6.5) where an analogous
L^{p}
result is proven from the
L^{2}

version using the good-
\lambda
technique. We record it here:

(4.11)

\|A(H)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\leq C\|S(H)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}+C\|\mu\|_{C}^{1/2}\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}.

5.
N<S_{p}
estimates
