, we integrate by parts in
x_{j}
to get

\frac{1}{2}\iint\partial_{j}\left(\frac{a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}\right)w_{k}^{2}\Psi^{m}dX\,dt+\frac{m}{2}\iint\left(\frac{a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}\right)w_{k}^{2}\partial_{j}\Psi\,\Psi^{m-1}dX\,dt+\int w_{k}^{2}\Psi^{m}dx\,dt=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

The term
I_{2}
is controlled by
C\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}\int\left|N(\Psi^{\frac{m-1}{2}}w_{k})\right|^{2}dx\,dt
since
|\nabla\Psi|dX\,dt
is a Carleson measure. The term
I_{3}
only arises when
j=n
and can be controlled by a similar bound. For the term
I_{1}
, observe that
j<n
, and introducing the term
\partial_{n}x_{n}
, we write:

I_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j<n}\iint\partial_{j}\left(\frac{a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}\right)w_{k}^{2}\Psi^{m}\partial_{n}x_{n}\,dX\,dt.

We then integrate by parts in
\partial_{n}
and get three terms: (1) when
\partial_{n}
hits
w_{k}^{2}
, we use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the assumption that
|\partial_{j}A|^{2}x_{n}dX\,dt
is a Carleson measure to bound the term by allowable quantities

\varepsilon\iint\left|\nabla w_{k}\right|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}\int\left|N(\Psi^{\frac{m}{2}}w_{k})\right|^{2}dx\,dt
;
(2) when
\partial_{n}
hits
\Psi^{m}
, we use the bound
|\nabla A(X,t)|x_{n}\lesssim 1
and the assumption that
|\nabla\Psi|dX\,dt
is a Carleson measure to bound the term by
C\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}\int\left|N(\Psi^{\frac{m-1}{2}}w_{k})\right|^{2}dx\,dt
; and (3) when
\partial_{n}
hits
\partial_{j}\left(\frac{a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}\right)
, we integrate by parts again in
x_{j}
. Since
j<n
, this gives

-\sum_{j<n}\iint\partial_{n}\left(\frac{a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}\right)\partial_{j}w_{k}\,w_{k}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt-\frac{m}{2}\sum_{j<n}\iint\partial_{n}\left(\frac{a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}\right)w_{k}^{2}\partial_{j}\Psi\,\Psi^{m-1}x_{n}\,dX\,dt.

Observe that both terms can be estimated similarly as the previous two terms and are controlled by allowable quantities.

It remains to estimate the term
J
. Since

\divg\left(\frac{A}{a_{nn}}\nabla w_{k}\right)=\frac{1}{a_{nn}}\divg(A\nabla w_{k})-\frac{\nabla a_{nn}}{a_{nn}^{2}}\cdot(A\nabla w_{k})
,
we can use the PDE for
w_{k}
and get

J=\iint\frac{1}{a_{nn}}(\partial_{t}w_{k})w_{k}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt-\iint\frac{1}{a_{nn}}\divg\left((\partial_{k}A)\nabla u\right)w_{k}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\\
+\iint\frac{\nabla a_{nn}}{a_{nn}^{2}}(A\nabla w_{k})w_{k}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt=:J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}.

Note that
J_{3}
can be estimated using Cauchy-Schwarz and the Carleson condition on
\nabla A
as before and controlled by allowable quantities. For
J_{1}
, integrating by parts in
t
gives that

J_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}\iint\partial_{t}\left(a_{nn}^{-1}\right)w_{k}^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt-\frac{m}{2}\iint\frac{1}{a_{nn}}w_{k}^{2}(\partial_{t}\Psi)\Psi^{m-1}x_{n}=:J_{11}+J_{12}.

Since
|\partial_{t}\Psi|x_{n}dX\,dt
is a Carleson measure, we have

|J_{12}|\leq C\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}\int\left|N(\Psi^{\frac{m-1}{2}}w_{k})\right|^{2}dx\,dt
.
For
J_{11}
, we write
x_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{n}(x_{n}^{2})
and then integrate by parts in
x_{n}
. This process gives three terms. When
\partial_{n}
hits
w_{k}^{2}
and
\Psi^{m}
, the two corresponding terms can both be bounded by allowable quantities using that
|\partial_{t}a_{nn}|^{2}x_{n}^{3}dX\,dt
is a Carleson measure, that
|\partial_{t}a_{nn}|x_{n}^{2}\lesssim 1
and that
|\partial_{n}\Psi|dX\,dt
is a Carleson measure, respectively. When
\partial_{n}
hits
\partial_{t}\left(a_{nn}^{-1}\right)
, we integrate by parts again in
\partial_{t}
to get

\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{\iint(\partial_{t}w_{k})w_{k}\partial_{n}(a_{nn}^{-1})\Psi^{m}x_{n}^{2}\,dX\,dt}_{=:J_{11*}}+\frac{m}{4}\iint w_{k}^{2}\partial_{n}(a_{nn}^{-1})(\partial_{t}\Psi)\Psi^{m-1}x_{n}^{2}\,dX\,dt.

Note that the second term can be controlled by an allowable quantity using the bound
|\partial_{n}a_{nn}|x_{n}\lesssim 1
and the Carleson condition on
\partial_{t}\Psi
. We estimate
J_{11*}
using Cauchy-Schwarz:

(7.12)

|J_{11*}|\leq\left(\iint|\partial_{t}w_{k}|^{2}\Psi^{m+2}x_{n}^{3}dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint|\partial_{n}a_{nn}|^{2}x_{n}\,w_{k}^{2}\Psi^{m-2}dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}.

The second term in the product is bounded by
\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}\int_{\partial\Omega}N(\Psi^{\frac{m}{2}-1}\nabla u)^{2}dx\,dt
using the Carleson condition on
\partial_{n}a_{nn}
. To estimate the first term in the product, we do a Whitney decomposition of
\supp\Psi
and apply Lemma
4.1
to each of the Whitney cube
Q
. Since
\left\{2Q\right\}
has finite intersections, we deduce that

(7.13)

\iint|\partial_{t}w_{k}|^{2}\Psi^{m+2}x_{n}^{3}dX\,dt\lesssim\iint|\partial_{t}u|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+\iint|\partial_{t}A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}\Psi^{m+2}x_{n}^{3}dX\,dt\\
\lesssim\iint\left(|\nabla A|^{2}x_{n}+|\partial_{t}A|x_{n}^{3}\right)|\nabla u|^{2}\Psi^{m}dX\,dt+\iint|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\\
\lesssim\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}\int_{\partial\Omega}N(\Psi^{\frac{m}{2}}\nabla u)^{2}dx\,dt+\iint|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt

where we have used
|\partial_{t}u|^{2}\sim|\nabla A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}
by the PDE satisfied by
u
in the second inequality. Plugging these estimates into (
7.12
) gives

|J_{11*}|\leq\varepsilon\iint|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}\int_{\partial\Omega}N(\Psi^{\frac{m}{2}-1}\nabla u)^{2}dx\,dt.

We are left with the term
J_{2}
from
J
. By the divergence theorem,

J_{2}=\iint(\partial_{k}A)\nabla u\cdot\nabla(a_{nn}^{-1})w_{k}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+\iint\frac{\partial_{k}A}{a_{nn}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla w_{k}\left(\Psi^{m}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt\\
+m\iint\frac{\partial_{k}A}{a_{nn}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\Psi\left(w_{k}\Psi^{m-1}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt+\iint\frac{\partial_{k}a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}\partial_{j}u\left(w_{k}\Psi^{m}\right)dX\,dt=:\sum_{i=1}^{4}J_{2i}.

We have

|J_{21}|\lesssim\iint|\nabla A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\leq C\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}\int_{\partial\Omega}N\left(\Psi^{m/2}\nabla u\right)^{2}dx\,dt.

The terms
J_{22}
and
J_{23}
can be estimated similarly as the first two terms coming from
I_{1}
.

For the term
J_{24}
, we introduce
1=\partial_{n}(x_{n})
and then integrate by parts in
x_{n}
. This process gives 4 terms, when
\partial_{n}
hits
\frac{\partial_{k}a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}
,
\partial_{j}u
,
w_{k}
, and
\Psi^{m}
, respectively. We only explain the situation when
\partial_{n}
hits
\frac{\partial_{k}a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}
, as the other 3 terms are similar to the terms treated before. In this case, we have

\iint\partial_{n}\left(\frac{\partial_{k}a_{nj}}{a_{nn}}\right)\partial_{j}u\left(w_{k}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt=\iint\partial_{n}(a_{nn}^{-1})\partial_{k}a_{nj}\partial_{j}u\left(w_{k}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt\\
+\iint a_{nn}^{-1}(\partial_{n}\partial_{k}a_{nj})\partial_{j}u\left(w_{k}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt.

The first term on the right-hand side can be estimated as
J_{21}
, while for the second term, we integrate by parts again in
x_{k}
. Since
k<n
, this process gives 4 terms, each of which can be handled and controlled by the allowable quantities as in the previous step. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

To prove Lemma
7.8
, we use the PDE (
4.5
) satisfied by
H
. Recall that matrix
B
is defined as
B=A^{T}/a_{nn}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.8
.

We start by writing

\iint|\nabla H|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\lesssim\iint B\nabla H\cdot\nabla H\left(\Psi^{m}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt\\
=-\iint HB\nabla H\cdot\nabla\left(\Psi^{m}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt\underbrace{-\iint\divg(B\nabla H)H\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt}_{=:S}.

The first term on the right-hand side can be estimated as in the proof of Lemma
7.4
; in fact, the argument is slightly simpler here since

b_{nn}=1
. More precisely, we write by product rule

-\iint HB\nabla H\cdot\nabla\left(\Psi^{m}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt\\
=-m\iint HB\nabla H\cdot\nabla\Psi\left(\Psi^{m-1}x_{n}\right)dX\,dt\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2}\iint b_{nj}\partial_{j}\left(H^{2}\right)\Psi^{m}dX\,dt}_{=:I}.

Observe that the first term on the right-hand side is bounded by

\varepsilon\iint|\nabla H|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+C_{\varepsilon}\int N(\Psi^{m/2-1}H)^{2}
, which is allowable because
|H|\lesssim|\nabla u|
. The term
I
can be estimated similarly as the term
I
in the proof of Lemma
7.4
. Namely, we integrate by parts in
x_{j}
, and when
\partial_{j}
hits
b_{nj}
, we introduce
1=\partial_{n}x_{n}
and then integrate by parts in
x_{n}
. For the term that involves
\partial_{n}\partial_{j}b_{nj}
, we use the fact that
j<n
(because
b_{nn}=1
) to integrate by parts again in
x_{j}
. When
j=n
we again get a
boundary term involving
H^{2}
. We skip the rest of the details.

We now estimate the term
S
using the PDE (
4.5
).

S=\iint\frac{1}{a_{nn}}(\partial_{t}H)H\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+\iint TH\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt=:S_{1}+S_{2},

where
T
is defined as in (
4.5
).
The term
S_{1}
is similar to
J_{1}
in the proof of Lemma
7.4
. The difference is in the term

S_{11*}:=\iint(\partial_{t}H)H\partial_{n}(a_{nn}^{-1})\Psi^{m}x_{n}^{2}dX\,dt,

which corresponds to
J_{11*}
in the proof of Lemma
7.4
. As in (
7.12
), we need to estimate
\iint|\partial_{t}H|^{2}\Psi^{m+2}x_{n}^{3}dX\,dt
. Since
H=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{nj}\partial_{j}u
, the product rule gives that

|\partial_{t}H|\lesssim|\partial_{t}A||\nabla u|+|\partial_{t}\nabla u|
. So

\iint|\partial_{t}H|^{2}\Psi^{m+2}x_{n}^{3}dX\,dt\lesssim\iint|\partial_{t}A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}^{3}dX\,dt+\iint|\partial_{t}\nabla u|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}^{3}dX\,dt,

where the first term is further bounded by
\int N\left(\Psi^{m/2}\nabla u\right)^{2}dx\,dt
, and the second term can be estimated exactly as (
7.13
). Hence, we conclude that
S_{1}
can be controlled by allowable quantities.

It remains to estimate
S_{2}
, which we further split into 3 terms: we write
S_{2}=S_{21}+S_{22}+S_{23}
, where

S_{21}:=\iint(\partial_{i}b_{in})\partial_{t}u\,H\Psi^{m}x_{n}dX\,dt,

S_{22}:=\iint\left[(\partial_{t}a_{nn}^{-1})H-(\partial_{t}b_{in})\partial_{i}u\right]H\Psi^{m}x_{n}dX\,dt,

and

S_{23}:=\iint\sum_{j<n}\left[\partial_{i}(b_{ij}\partial_{j}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u))-\partial_{k}(b_{kn}\partial_{j}(a_{ji}\partial_{i}u))\right]H\Psi^{m}x_{n}dX\,dt.

For
S_{21}
, we use the PDE to get that
|\partial_{t}u|\lesssim|\nabla A||\nabla u|+|\nabla^{2}u|
, and hence

|S_{21}|\lesssim\iint|\nabla A|^{2}|\nabla u|H\Psi^{m}x_{n}dX\,dt+\iint|\nabla A||\nabla^{2}u|H\Psi^{m}x_{n}\\
\lesssim(1+C_{\varepsilon})\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}\int N\left(\Psi^{m/2}|\nabla u|\right)^{2}dX\,dt+\varepsilon\iint|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}.

To estimate
S_{22}
, we write
x_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{n}\left(x_{n}^{2}\right)
and integrate by parts in
x_{n}
. This yields 3 terms. When
\partial_{n}
hits
H
or
\Psi^{m}
, we can control the corresponding terms by allowable quantities using the Carleson condition on
|\partial_{t}A|
(and also on
\partial_{n}\Psi
when
\partial_{n}
hits
\Psi^{m}
) and Hölder inequality. When
\partial_{n}
hits the quantity in the bracket
[\quad]
, we essentially need to treat differently (when
\partial_{n}
hits on the
H
or
\partial_{i}u
in the bracket, it yields terms similar as before)

\iint\partial_{n}\partial_{t}(a_{nn}^{-1})\,H^{2}\Psi^{m}x_{n}^{2}dX\,dt-\iint(\partial_{n}\partial_{t}b_{in})\partial_{i}u\,H\,x_{n}^{2}dX\,dt.

We integrate by parts again in
t
for each integral, which yields 4 terms. All of these terms are similar to the terms that we have treated already, namely,
S_{11*}
, and
J_{11*}
as well as
J_{23}
in the proof of Lemma
7.4
. So
|S_{22}|
can be bounded by allowable quantities.

It remains to estimate
S_{23}
. There are two types of terms: (i) there are two derivatives falling on the coefficients
a_{ij}
or
b_{ij}
, and (ii) terms where no two derivatives fall on the coefficients. In case (i), observe that one of the two derivatives must involve
\partial_{j}
, which is never
\partial_{n}
. So for these terms, we integrate by parts in
x_{j}
and obtain terms of type (ii) as well as

S_{231}:=-\sum_{j<n}\iint\left[b_{ij}\partial_{i}(a_{nk}\partial_{k}u)-b_{kn}\partial_{k}(a_{ji}\partial_{i}u)\right]\partial_{j}\left(H\Psi^{m}\right)x_{n}dX\,dt.

We let the reader check that all the terms from
S_{231}
are similar to terms that we have already treated.
For terms of type (ii), observe that since the terms with 3 derivatives on
u
cancel, there are only terms that can be bounded by (up to a multiplicative constant)

\iint|\nabla A|^{2}|\nabla u||H|\Psi^{m}x_{n}\,dX\,dt,

which is bounded by
C\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}\int N\left(\Psi^{m/2}\nabla u\right)^{2}dx\,dt
.

This completes the proof of Lemma
7.8
.
∎

8.
Localization on bounded Lipschitz cylinders

In this section we introduce a localization technique that will allow us to establish solvability of the Neumann problem on Lipschitz cylinders of the form
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
for a bounded Lipschitz domain

\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
(with sufficiently small Lipschitz norm) using previously established solvability on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. This proves Theorem
1.6
for bounded Lipschitz cylinders.
We note that this localization is not needed in the case when
\mathcal{O}
is an unbounded Lipschitz domain
of the form
\{(x,x_{n}):\,x_{n}>\phi(x)\}
for a Lipschitz function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
, as in such a case we can consider a pullback map
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
and transfer the parabolic PDE from
\Omega
to

\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
directly without any localization. We will return to this point when we discuss the map
\rho

in more detail where we will show how to solve the unbounded case above such Lipschitz graph.

In what follows, we (in rough outline) borrow ideas from Section 11 of
[
14
]
where a similar decomposition was considered as well as
[
16
]
. Recalling the definition of the Lipschitz domain

\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
from Definition
2.2
we restrict ourselves to the case

\diam(\mathcal{O})<\infty
.

A simple compactness argument implies that there exists
s_{0}<1
such that the
\ell
-cylinders
s_{0}{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
still cover
\partial\mathcal{O}
. Thus by making the scale
r_{0}
and diameter
d
smaller if necessary (at the expense of increased
N
) we may without loss of generality assume that the union of
(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
covers
\partial\mathcal{O}
.

By rescaling the PDE, it may also be assumed that the
\ell
-cylinders
{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
in the definition above have diameter
d=1
. Let
N,\,C_{0}
be as above and hence
there are
N
such
\ell
-cylinders
(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
needed to cover the boundary
\partial\mathcal{O}
.
It follows that there exists a partition of unity
(\varphi_{j})_{j=1}^{N}
such that each
\varphi_{j}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, is supported in

(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
, and altogether,

\sum_{j=1}^{N}\varphi_{j}(x)=1,\qquad\mbox{ for all }\qquad x\in\left\{x\in\mathcal{O}:\,\mathrm{dist}\left(x,{\partial\mathcal{O}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\right\}.

As each
\varphi_{j}
is smooth, and there are
N
of them, it follows that
\|\nabla\varphi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq M
for some
M>0
for all
j
. This completes
the description of the partition of the spatial component of our domain
\Omega
.

Fix a scale
r_{0}\in(0,1/2]
to be specified later, let
\Delta_{i}=\partial\mathcal{O}\times(ir_{0}^{2},(i+1)r_{0}^{2}]
for
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. There exists a nonnegative
C^{1}
function
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, supp
\psi\subset[0,3r_{0}^{2}]
such that for

\psi_{i}(\cdot)=\psi(\cdot+ir_{0}^{2}),\qquad|\psi^{\prime}|\lesssim r_{0}^{-2},\qquad\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\psi_{i}\equiv 1.

Consider now an arbitrary
g\in{L}^{p}(\partial\Omega)
for some
p>1
, and the solution to
\mathcal{L}u=0
with
\partial_{\nu}^{A}u=\langle A\nabla u\cdot\nu\rangle\big|_{\partial\Omega}=g
.

For all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
j\in\{1,2,\dots,N\}
we denote by
v_{ij}
the solution of the boundary value problem

(8.1)

\begin{cases}\,\,\,\mathcal{L}v_{ij}=0,&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{\nu}^{A}v_{ij}=g\psi_{i}\varphi_{j}:=g_{ij}&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Clearly, since
\sum_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\psi_{i}\varphi_{j}=1
it follows that

u=\sum_{i,j}v_{ij}
solves
\mathcal{L}u=0

with Neumann boundary data
g
.

Observe that the boundary data of
v_{ij}
is supported on
(\partial\mathcal{O}\cap(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j})\times(ir_{0}^{2},(i+3)r_{0}^{2}]
and hence in
\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2}
. Furthermore we have that

\|g_{ij}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}.

Let us fix
i,j
and consider
v=v_{ij}
, where for convenience we drop the indices
i,j
.
Without loss of generality (by shifting the time variable) and relabeling the
\ell
-cylinders
{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
we may assume that
\partial_{\nu}^{A}v
is supported in
(\partial\mathcal{O}\cap(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{1})\times(-6r_{0}^{2},-3r_{0}^{2})
and let
us call
h
the datum on this set.

We first state a key theorem from
[
15
]
:

Theorem 8.2
.

[
15
, Theorem 1.4]

Let
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
where
\Omega
is either a bounded or unbounded Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{t}+\divg(A\nabla\cdot)
, and let
\mathcal{L}^{*}=\partial_{t}+\divg(A^{T}\nabla\cdot)
be the adjoint operator of
\mathcal{L}
.
Let
p\in(1,\infty)
. Suppose that
(N_{\mathcal{L}})_{p}
and
(D_{\mathcal{L}^{*}})_{p^{\prime}}
are solvable in
\Omega
. Then for any backward parabolic cube
J_{r}=J_{r}(x,t):=Q_{r}(x)\times(t-r^{2},t)
centered at some
(x,t)\in\partial\Omega
, and any weak local solution in
J_{2r}\cap\Omega
to
\mathcal{L}u=0
with zero Neumann data on
J_{2r}\cap\partial\Omega
, we have

(8.3)

\|\widetilde{N}(|\nabla u|{\mathds{1}}_{J_{r}})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq Cr^{(n+1)/p}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{J_{2r}\cap\Omega}|\nabla u|dX\,dt.

Here we have stated the theorem in its
L^{1}
version instead of the
L^{2}
version in the paper, but one follows from the other using boundary reverse Hölder’s inequality for the gradient which in turn follows from the boundary Caccioppoli inequality.
The theorem above is useful in that it applies to local solutions with zero Neumann data. This allows us to verify the assumptions of this result (solvability) on a different domain which is unbounded and then apply it to our case of a bounded domain. To explain this more fully, consider a parabolic ball that sits inside one of the coordinate patches of
\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
.
In the local coordinates of this particular patch on
8{\mathbb{Z}}
we may assume the following:

\phi:\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}:\,|x|\leq 8\}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Lipschitz function with Lipschitz constant
\ell\leq\ell(p)
with
\phi(0)=0

and in the coordinates
(x,x_{n},t)
the portion of domain
\Omega
that belongs to
8{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{R}
can be written
as

\Omega\cap(8{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{R})=\{(x,x_{n},t):\,|x|\leq 8,\,\phi(x)<x_{n}<8(\ell+1),\,t\in\mathbb{R}\},

with
\partial\Omega\cap(8{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{R})
being the graph of function
\phi
times
\mathbb{R}
.
Let us now choose a new function
\tilde{\phi}:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
with Lipschitz constant at most
3\ell
having the following properties:

\tilde{\phi}(x)={\phi}(x)\quad\mbox{for all }|x|\leq 6,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\tilde{\phi}(x)=0\quad\mbox{for all }|x|\geq 8.

This can be done trivially by multiplying
\phi
by a cutoff function.

We similarly extend the coefficients of our equation. The original parabolic PDE for a solution
v
to
\mathcal{L}v=0
on
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
can be written in the above coordinates as

-\partial_{t}v+\divg(A\nabla v)=0,\qquad\mbox{where $A$ is uniformly elliptic in }[8{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R},

and has coefficients satisfying the Carleson condition, with
\|\mu\|_{C}\leq\mu(p)
.

We would like to extend the PDE to the domain
\tilde{\Omega}:=\{(x,x_{n},t):\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\,x_{n}>\tilde{\phi}(x),\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
such that
the PDE will coincide with the original PDE for
v
in the set
[4{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R}
, and so that the new matrix
\tilde{A}

will be uniformly elliptic and satisfy the Carleson condition with respect to the boundary of
the domain
\partial\tilde{\Omega}
.
This is easily accomplished via a partition of unity
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
subordinate to the
cover of
\{(x,x_{n}):\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\,x_{n}>\tilde{\phi}(x)\}
by the sets
6{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus[4{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}]
with new matrix
\tilde{A}
defined by

\eta_{1}(x,x_{n})A(x,x_{n},t)+\eta_{2}(x,x_{n})I,\qquad\mbox{for all }\{(x,x_{n},t):\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\,x_{n}>\tilde{\phi}(x),\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}.

When
|x|\leq 4
and
x_{n}<4(\ell+1)
this preserves the property that
\tilde{A}=A
, while away from
|x|\geq 8
or
x_{n}>8(\ell+1)
the matrix is simply the identity matrix and thus trivially satisfies both ellipticity and Carleson condition. The size of the Carleson condition of the new matrix
\tilde{A}
is bounded by
10\mu(p)
.

It follows that the solution
v
from Theorem
8.2
which is defined on
J_{2r}\cap\Omega

can be also seen as a solution of the new PDE with matrix
\tilde{A}
on the domain
\tilde{\Omega}
.
This is an unbounded domain above a graph of a Lipschitz function and hence the solvability of the Neumann problem
(N_{\mathcal{L}})_{p}
and the Dirichlet problem of its adjoint
(D_{\mathcal{L}^{*}})_{p^{\prime}}
in
\tilde{\Omega}
can be deduced from the solvability
of these two problems on the domain
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. To see this consider
a bijective bi-Lipschitz map
\Psi:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\to\tilde{\mathcal{O}}=\{(x,x_{n}):\,x_{n}>\tilde{\phi}(x)\}
.
The natural choice here is the map due to Dahlberg, Kenig, Nečas, Stein
(see for example
[
5
]
or
[
27
]
) defined as

(8.4)

\Psi(x,x_{n})=(x,c_{0}x_{n}+(\theta_{x_{n}}*\tilde{\phi})(x)),

where
(\theta_{s})_{s>0}
is smooth compactly supported approximate
identity and
c_{0}
can be chosen large enough (depending only on

\|\nabla\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1})}
) so that
\Psi
is
one to one.

Extend this map to

(8.5)

\rho(x,x_{n},t)=(\Psi(x,x_{n}),t),

a map from
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
to
\tilde{\Omega}
, the domain on which we consider the PDE

-\partial_{t}u+\divg(\tilde{A}\nabla u)=0
.

Then

U=u\circ\rho,\quad\text{solves the parabolic PDE}\quad\mathcal{L}_{1}U=0\text{ on }{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}

where

\mathcal{L}_{1}=-\partial_{t}+\divg(B\nabla\cdot),

where
B
is a new matrix obtained from the original matrix
\tilde{A}
.

This type of mapping has been studied extensively, in the parabolic setting in
[
21
]
. However, the above mapping is simpler than the one in
[
21
]

as it is constant in
t
. Crucially, as shown in
[
21
]
, the new matrix
B
above inherits the Carleson condition that
\tilde{A}
satisfies with perhaps a larger constant (as the Carleson norm also depends on
\|\nabla\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1})}
and thus the Carleson norm of coefficients
B
depends both on
\ell(p)
and
\mu(p)
). Crucially, if
\ell(p)
and
\mu(p)
are small then so is the Carleson norm of coefficients
B
. Furthermore as
\Psi
is
t
-independent, the above change of variables does not introduce any first order (drift) term. Finally, we observe that the ellipticity of the matrix
B
follows from the ellipticity of
A
and
I
.

It follows that the PDE for
U
is of the type considered in Theorem
1.6
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}

for which the solvability of the Neumann problem has been established.
Applying Theorem
1.6
for
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
to the operator
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, we have that
for any
1<p<\infty
,
the
L^{p}
Neumann problem for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is solvable on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
if the Carleson norm of the matrix
B
is sufficiently small (i.e., if
\ell(p)
and
\mu(p)
are sufficiently small). Specifically, for some
C=C(A,\lambda,\Lambda,\ell,n,p)>0
,

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla U)\|_{L^{p}(\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}))}\leq C\|\widetilde{g}\|_{{L}^{p}(\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}))},

for Neumann datum
\widetilde{g}
of
U
.

As
U=u\circ\rho
where
\rho
is bi-Lipschitz the same estimate also holds for
u
on the domain
\tilde{\Omega}
. Thus
(N_{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}})_{p}
is solvable on
\tilde{\Omega}
.
Notice that the assumption that
(D_{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{*}})_{p^{\prime}}
is solvable is satisfied because under the above conditions on the matrix
A
the small Carleson case was fully resolved in
[
9
,
11
]
and thus we may apply the result of Theorem
8.2
for
v
with zero Neumann data on
J_{2r}\cap\tilde{\Omega}
. But on

J_{2r}\cap\tilde{\Omega}=J_{2r}\cap{\Omega}
we have
A=\tilde{A}
and hence (
8.3
) does apply to
our
v
on
J_{2r}\cap\Omega
. We also note that because of the interior regularity of coefficients
(|\nabla A|\lesssim\delta(X)^{-1})
we can here and below replace the averaged version of the nontangential maximal function
\tilde{N}
by its stronger sup-version
N
. We do that from now onwards.

Equipped with this, given that
\partial_{\nu}^{A}v=0
when
t<-6r_{0}^{2}
we know that
v
must be constant for all
t<-6r_{0}^{2}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
v=0
when
t<-3<-6r_{0}^{2}
. Let

J_{j}=(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\times(-3,0),\quad\mbox{and hence }\quad 2J_{j}={\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\times(-6,0),

j=1,2,\dots,N
. Recall, that the scale of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
is comparable to
O(1)
and hence we may apply Theorem

8.2
to each such
J_{j}
with
r
in (
8.3
) taken to be
1
. It follows that there exists
p_{0}=p_{0}(\lambda,\Lambda,n,\|\mu\|_{C},\ell)>1
such that for all
1<p<p_{0}
we have that

(8.6)

\|N(|\nabla v|{\mathds{1}}_{J_{j}})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C_{p}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{2J_{j}\cap\Omega}|\nabla v|dX\,dt,\qquad j=2,3,\dots,N.

Obviously, the estimate fails to hold when
j=1
as our Neumann data
h
is supported on
J_{1}\cap\partial\Omega
.

We recall the earlier definition of coefficients
\tilde{A}

and domain
\tilde{\Omega}
such that on the set
[4{\mathbb{Z}}_{1}\cap\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R}
the PDEs

-\partial_{t}v+\divg(A\nabla v)=0
and
-\partial_{t}w+\divg(\tilde{A}\nabla w)=0
coincide and also

[4{\mathbb{Z}}_{1}\cap\partial\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R}=[4{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\partial\tilde{\mathcal{O}}]\times\mathbb{R}.

In reality,
\tilde{\Omega}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\tilde{A}
depends on the index
j
but we suppress this dependence as
j
is fixed (and relabeled to be
1
).

Let us denote by
w
the solution of the PDE problem

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}w+\divg(\tilde{A}\nabla w)=0,&\text{in }\tilde{\Omega},\\
\partial_{\nu}^{A}w=h&\text{on }\partial\tilde{\Omega}.\end{cases}

It follows that
v
and
w
have the same non-vanishing Neumann data on the portion of parabolic boundary they share (equal to
h
) and zero Neumann data elsewhere.
Hence,
v-w
, which is well defined on
[4{\mathbb{Z}}_{1}\cap\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R}
has
vanishing Neumann data on
[4{\mathbb{Z}}_{1}\cap\partial\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R}
and therefore we also have

(8.7)

\|N(|\nabla(v-w)|{\mathds{1}}_{J_{1}})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{2J_{1}\cap\Omega}|\nabla(v-w)|dX\,dt.

From the assumed Neumann solvability for the PDE for
w
on the domain
\tilde{\Omega}
for
1<p\leq p_{0}

we get that

(8.8)

\|N(\nabla w{\mathds{1}}_{2J_{1}})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\tilde{\Omega})}\leq C\|h\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

Thus the
\nabla w
part in (
8.7
) is bounded by
C\|h\|_{L^{p}}
.
From (
8.6
)-(
8.8
) and the fact that
v
and
w
vanish for
t<-6r_{0}^{2}
, we arrive at:

(8.9)

\|N((\nabla v){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|h\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+C\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times(-6r_{0}^{2},0)}|\nabla v|.

Here we have removed the averages from the integral as the averaging happens over the sets of parabolic volume of size
O(1)
.

Let

\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}\cap\{X:\,d(X,\partial\mathcal{O})>\varepsilon\}.

Since trivially,

\iint_{(\mathcal{O}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon})\times(-3,0)}|\nabla v|\lesssim\varepsilon\|N((\nabla v){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{O}\times(-3,0)})\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\varepsilon\|N((\nabla v){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)},

it follows that we can fix
\varepsilon>0
small such that the estimate

(8.10)

\|N((\nabla v){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq 2C\|h\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+C\iint_{\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\times(-6r_{0}^{2},0)}|\nabla v|.

holds for all
v
we consider with constant only depending on the coefficients of our PDE and the Lipschitz constant of the graph.

8.1.
Higher order nontangential maximal functions.

We recall the result of Lemma
4.1
which established Caccioppoli inequality for
\nabla\partial_{t}u
. Having this it follows that for every
(X,t)\in\partial\Omega
(under the assumption on coefficients of
A
:
|\nabla A|\lesssim\delta^{-1}
,
|\partial_{t}A|\lesssim\delta^{-2}
)

(8.11)

\widetilde{N}_{\alpha/2}(\delta(\cdot)^{-2}\partial_{t}\nabla u)(X,t)\lesssim\widetilde{N}_{\alpha}(\nabla u)(X,t),

where
\delta
, as before denotes the parabolic distance of a point to the boundary
\partial\Omega
.
We would prefer to work with pointwise nontangential maximal functions (to avoid
L^{2}
averaging over interior sets). Clearly, under the assumption
|\nabla A|\lesssim\delta^{-1}
, the
\nabla u
is well-defined pointwise, which follows from the De Giorgi-Nash-Moser estimates for the PDE for
\partial_{i}u
. This implies

N_{\alpha/2}(\nabla u)(X,t)\lesssim\widetilde{N}_{\alpha}(\nabla u)(X,t).

Then using the PDE for
w=\partial_{t}u
, namely (as stated previously)

-\partial_{t}w+\divg(A\nabla w)=-\divg((\partial_{t}A)\nabla u),

it again follows by De Giorgi-Nash-Moser estimates that under the additional assumption of

|\partial_{t}A|\lesssim\delta^{-2}
we also have
\partial_{t}u\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\Omega)
and hence also
\nabla^{2}u\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\Omega)
(using the PDE for
u
).

Differentiating the above PDE again with respect to
\partial_{i}
,
i=1,2,\dots,n
we get for
v_{i}=\partial_{t}\partial_{i}u
:

-\partial_{t}v_{i}+\divg(A\nabla v_{i})=-\divg((\partial_{i}A)\nabla w)-\divg((\partial_{t}A)\nabla v_{i})-\divg((\partial_{i}\partial_{t}A)\nabla u),

from which (under the assumption
|\nabla\partial_{t}A|\lesssim\delta^{-3}
) we can then deduce that
\partial_{t}\nabla u\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\Omega)
.
Obviously this can be iterated ad infinitum, but for our purposes it suffices to record that under the assumptions
|\nabla A|\lesssim\delta^{-1}
,
|\partial_{t}A|\lesssim\delta^{-2}
, and
|\nabla\partial_{t}A|\lesssim\delta^{-3}
we have a well-defined
\partial_{t}\nabla u
(locally bounded) and (
8.11
) improves to the inequality

(8.12)

N_{\alpha/2}(\delta(\cdot)^{-2}\partial_{t}\nabla u)(X,t)\lesssim N_{\alpha}(\nabla u)(X,t).

We now apply this for
v
as in (
8.10
). Since
v
vanishes on
(-\infty,-6r_{0}^{2}]
we have

\nabla v=0
at the time
-6r_{0}^{2}
and hence by the fundamental theorem of calculus

\int_{-6r_{0}^{2}}^{0}|\nabla v|\,dt\leq 6r_{0}^{2}\int_{-6r_{0}^{2}}^{0}|\partial_{t}\nabla v|\,dt.

We now integrate both sides over
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
and use (
8.12
) to conclude that

(8.13)

\iint_{\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\times(-6r_{0}^{2},0)}|\nabla v|\leq Cr_{0}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{\partial\mathcal{O}\times(-6r_{0}^{2},0)}N((\nabla v)1_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)}).

8.2.
Solvability for small
r_{0}

We are now ready to combine (
8.13
) and (
8.10
). Since
\varepsilon>0
was fixed earlier we get that

(8.14)

\|N((\nabla v){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq 2C\|h\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+Cr_{0}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}\int_{\partial\mathcal{O}\times(-6r_{0}^{2},0)}N((\nabla v)1_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)})\\
\leq 2C\|h\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+C_{\varepsilon,p}r_{0}^{2}\|N((\nabla v){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

Hence if
r_{0}\in(0,1/2]
is chosen small enough such that
C_{\varepsilon,p}r_{0}^{2}\leq 1/2

the last term of (
8.14
) can be absorbed by the left-hand side and we obtain that

(8.15)

\|N((\nabla v){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq 4C\|h\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

This is the desired estimate for
v
with nonvanishing data in
(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\times(-6r_{0}^{2},-3r_{0}^{2})
.
From now on, we consider
r_{0}
fixed, chosen such that
r_{0}=(2C_{\varepsilon,p})^{-1/2}
.

The next ingredient we introduce is the exponential decay of
\nabla v
on parts of the parabolic cylinder
where the Neumann data vanish. The following is proven in
[
16
]
:

Proposition 8.16
.

Let
u
be such that
{L}u=0
on
\partial\mathcal{O}\times[0,\infty)
and
moreover suppose that
\partial_{\nu}^{A}u=0
on
\partial\mathcal{O}\times[0,\infty)
.
Then there exists a
\beta>0
such that for all
t>0
and
c=\fint_{\mathcal{O}}u(\cdot,0)

(8.17)

\displaystyle\|u(\cdot,t)-c\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}\lesssim e^{-\beta t}\|u(\cdot,0)-c\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}\lesssim e^{-\beta t}\|\nabla u(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}.

For purposes that will soon become clear, we introduce a modified nontangential maximal function which we denote by
{N}^{r_{0}}
. In any
L^{p}
norm,
{N}
and
{N}^{r_{0}}
are equivalent, with a constant depending on
r_{0}
(which was already fixed and hence it suffices to work with
{N}^{r_{0}}
).

For a point
(P,\tau)\in\partial\Omega
let
\Gamma(P,\tau)
be the usual nontangential cone of some fixed aperture. Let

\Gamma^{r_{0}}(P,\tau)=\Gamma(P,\tau)\cap\{(X,t)\in\Omega:\delta(X,t)\leq r_{0}\},

I^{r_{0}}(P,\tau)=\{X\in\mathcal{O}:d(X,\partial\mathcal{O})>r_{0}\}\times(\tau-r_{0}^{2},\tau+r_{0}^{2}).

For a locally bounded function
v:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
let

{N}^{r_{0}}(v)(P,\tau)=\sup_{(X,t)\in\Gamma^{r_{0}}(P,\tau)}|v^{r_{0}}(X,t)|+\sup_{(X,t)\in I^{r_{0}}(P,\tau)}|v^{r_{0}}(X,t)|.

Observe that the main difference from the usual nontangential maximal function is that we limit the maximal
width of nontangential cones (in time) to
r_{0}^{2}
and hence for a point
(P,\tau)\in\partial\Omega
the value of
{N}^{r_{0}}(v)(P,\tau)
will depend on values of
v

only for time in the set
\mathcal{O}\times(\tau-r_{0}^{2},\tau+r_{0}^{2})
, i.e., a set whose width in the time variable is proportional to
r_{0}^{2}
.

Let us now return to our solution
v
with Neumann data supported in
(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\times(-6r_{0}^{2},-3r_{0}^{2})
.
Notice that thanks to the estimate (
8.15
) we have,
for
\Delta_{i}=\partial\mathcal{O}\times(ir_{0}^{2},(i+1)r_{0}^{2}]
,
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, that

(8.18)

\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{-2})}\leq\|N((\nabla v){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{O}\times(-\infty,0)})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq 4C\|h\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

We claim the following lemma holds (see also
[
13
, Lemma 5.10]
for a version of this lemma that applies to the Dirichlet problem and
[
16
, Lemma 4.3]
for an
L^{1}
Neumann problem version under different assumptions):

Lemma 8.19
.

Let
p>1
. There exists
\ell(p)>0
and
\mu(p)>0

such that if

•

the Carleson norm
\mu
of the coefficients of
\mathcal{L}u=-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
on
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\|\mu\|_{C}\leq\mu(p)
,

•

for each
j=1,2,\dots,N
we have
\ell\leq\ell(p)
in the Definition
2.2
, i.e.,
locally the Lipschitz norm of the graph describing
\partial\mathcal{O}
is sufficiently small.

Let
v
be a solution to
\mathcal{L}v=0
and
suppose that
\partial^{A}_{\nu}v=0
on
\partial\mathcal{O}\times(ir_{0}^{2},\infty)
for some
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
There exists an
\alpha=\alpha(r_{0})>0
such that for all
k>i
we have that

\displaystyle\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{k})}\lesssim e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}.

Proof.

Assume that
\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}<\infty
and that
\partial_{\nu}^{A}v\big|_{\partial\Omega}\equiv 0
for all times
\geq ir_{0}^{2}
. Let
\Omega_{i}:=\mathcal{O}\times(ir_{0}^{2},(i+1)r_{0}^{2}]
.
Clearly, then

\int_{\Omega_{i}}|\nabla v|\lesssim\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{1}(\Delta_{i})}\lesssim_{r_{0}}\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}.

Hence using the condition
\partial_{\nu}^{A}v=0
for
\tau>ir_{0}^{2}
and
L^{2}
integrability of the gradient (by boundary Caccioppoli inequality we get that for some
\tau\in(ir_{0}^{2},(i+1)r_{0}^{2})

\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}\times\{\tau\}}|\nabla v|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}<\infty.

Hence, by Sobolev embedding on
\mathcal{O}\times\{\tau\}
(for
c
as in Proposition
8.16
)

\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}\times\{\tau\}}|v-c|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}<\infty.

Fix now some
k>i+3
. We propagate the
L^{2}
initial data given on
\mathcal{O}\times\{\tau\}
using Proposition
8.16
to
t\in((k-3)r_{0}^{2},(k+4)r_{0}^{2})
. It follows that for all such
t
we have

\|v(\cdot,t)-c\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}\lesssim e^{-\beta r_{0}^{2}(k-i-4)}\|v(\cdot,\tau)-c\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}\lesssim e^{-\beta r_{0}^{2}|i-k|}\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}<\infty.

By integrating over the interval
((k-3)r_{0}^{2},(k+4)r_{0}^{2})
and then applying boundary Hölder regularity for solutions

(8.20)

\sup_{\Omega_{k-2}\cup\Omega_{k-1}\cup\Omega_{k}\cup\Omega_{k+1}\cup\Omega_{k+2}}|v-c|\lesssim e^{-\beta r_{0}^{2}|i-k|}\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}<\infty.

Observe that, by boundary Caccioppoli inequality, the
\sup_{\Omega_{k-2}\cup\Omega_{k-1}\cup\Omega_{k}\cup\Omega_{k+1}\cup\Omega_{k+2}}|v-c|

controls the
L^{2}
norm of
\nabla v
over the smaller set
\mathcal{O}\times((k-1)r_{0}^{2},(k+2)r_{0}^{2})
. Applying
(
8.3
) over a covering of
\partial\mathcal{O}\times(kr_{0}^{2},(k+1)r_{0}^{2})
by backwards parabolic cubes
J
as in Theorem
8.2
, we have for all
k>i+3
:

\int_{\Delta_{k}}{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)^{p}\lesssim e^{-\alpha p|i-k|}\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}^{p},

which is our claim. Here we are using the assumptions of Lemma
8.19
(smallness of
\mu(p)
and
\ell(p)
) which allows us to verify the assumptions of Theorem
8.2
on an unbounded domain; i.e., we use the process described in detail below Theorem
8.2
.

The fact that our claim holds for
k>i
(and not just
k>i+3
as proven) can be seen by using the maximum principle in this region (where there is no decay).
∎

In particular, we can apply Lemma
8.19
to each
v_{ij}
defined by (
8.1
).
It follows that

(8.21)

\int_{\Delta_{k}}{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v_{ij})^{p}\lesssim e^{-\alpha p(k-i)}\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i+4})}^{p},\qquad\mbox{for all }k=i+5,i+6,\dots.

Meanwhile, (
8.15
) means that for each
v_{ij}
we have

(8.22)

\sum_{k=i-1}^{i+4}\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{k})}^{p}\lesssim\|g_{ij}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\|g\psi_{i}\varphi_{j}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}.

And since
v_{ij}\equiv 0
for
t<ir_{0}^{2}
it follows that
\|{N}^{r_{0}}(\nabla v_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{k})}=0
when
k<i-1
.

We are ready to add up the estimates to obtain a bound for the
L^{p}
norm of
u
. Fix
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}

and consider
N^{r_{0}}(\nabla u)
on
\Delta_{i}
. Since
N^{r_{0}}
is sub-additive, clearly

N^{r_{0}}(\nabla u)(P,\tau)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k}\left[N^{r_{0}}(\nabla v_{kj})\right](P,\tau),

and therefore

\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\leq\sum_{j,k}\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla v_{kj})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\\
\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\sum_{k=i-4}^{i+1}\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla v_{kj})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}+\sum_{k<i-4}\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla v_{kj})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\right)\\
\lesssim_{N}\sum_{k=i-4}^{i+1}\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{k}\cup\Delta_{k+1}\cup\Delta_{k+2})}+\sum_{k<i-4}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})},

using (
8.21
) and (
8.22
). We raise both sides to the
p
-th power. It follows that

\hskip-42.67912pt\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\lesssim\sum_{k=i-4}^{i+1}\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}+\left(\sum_{k<i-4}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}\right)^{p}.

For the last term we use Hölder’s inequality for series. We split the exponential into two terms

e^{-\alpha|i-k|}=e^{-\alpha|i-k|/p^{\prime}}e^{-\alpha|i-k|/p}

to obtain

\left(\sum_{k<i-4}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}\right)^{p}\leq\left(\sum_{k<i-4}\left(e^{-\alpha|i-k|/p^{\prime}}\right)^{p^{\prime}}\right)^{p/p^{\prime}}\times\\
\qquad\qquad\sum_{k<i-4}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}^{p}\leq C(p,\alpha)\sum_{k<i-4}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\|g\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}.

Now we sum over all indices
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Since

\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}=\sum_{i}\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}
we obtain after rearranging the summation order:

(8.23)

\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(1+\sum_{k>4}e^{-\alpha k})\|g\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}.

We use the finite overlap of the sets
\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2}
for different values of
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
which allows us to claim that

\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\|g\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{i+1}\cup\Delta_{i+2})}\lesssim\|g\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

In summary, we have that

(8.24)

\|N(\nabla u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim_{r_{0}}\|N^{r_{0}}(\nabla u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|g\|^{p}_{{L}^{p}(\partial\Omega)},

proving solvability of the Neumann problem for this particular
p>1
. Hence we are done, as this argument extends the solvability result stated in Theorem
1.6
to
bounded Lipschitz cylinders. ∎

Appendix A
Remarks on the nontangential maximal function of
D^{1/2}_{t}u

Recall that in the paper
[
14
]
we have established nontangential estimates for
\tilde{N}(\nabla u)

and hence using the result of
[
8
]
, which states that

(A.1)

\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+\|\tilde{N}(H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}u\big|_{\partial\Omega}\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)},

for all solutions to
\mathcal{L}u=0
and all
1<p<\infty
, the
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
bounds follow for both

\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)
and
\tilde{N}(H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}u)
for the parabolic Regularity problem. Here
H_{t}
denotes the Hilbert transform in the
t
variable.
An analogous bound was shown in
[
2
]

where the
L^{2}
solvability was considered for the Regularity and Neumann problems for special matrices.

Hence, a natural question arises: Does the solution
u
, the existence of which is guaranteed by Theorem
1.6
, together with the bound for
\tilde{N}(\nabla u)
, also enjoy an estimate for
\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)
?

In this appendix we will show that for the Neumann problem the answer depends on whether the set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
in our domain
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
is bounded.

We prove that in the case where this set is bounded the answer is negative (i.e., such a bound does not exist) for all
1<p\leq\infty
. In the unbounded case we show that such estimates do hold in the range
1<p\leq 2
. This answer applies to both
\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)
and
\tilde{N}(H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}u)
by similar considerations. We focus here on

\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)
for simplicity, given the similarity of the two bounds.

A.1.
The bounded case

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. For any
1<p\leq\infty
we would like to prove impossibility
of the estimate

(A.2)

\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{O})\|g\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)},

for some fixed
C>0
. Here
u
solves
\mathcal{L}u=-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
in
\Omega
with
L^{p}
Neumann data
g
. We prove this by finding
L^{p}
boundary data
g
for which
\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)

is not an
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
function.

Assume that (
A.2
) holds for some
p\in(1,\infty]
. Consider an
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
function
g
to be specified later and let
u
solve
\mathcal{L}u=0
with the Neumann datum
g
. Clearly,
u
is determined up to a constant which we shall specify momentarily. Using the PDE for
u
we get after integrating over
\mathcal{O}
that

(A.3)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{O}}u\,dX=\int_{\mathcal{O}}\divg(A\nabla u)\,dX=\int_{\partial\mathcal{O}}\partial_{\nu}^{A}u\,d\sigma=\int_{\partial\mathcal{O}}g\,d\sigma.

Hence after integrating over a finite interval
[0,t]
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
we get that

v(t):=\int_{\mathcal{O}}u(X,t)\,dX=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial\mathcal{O}}g(x,\tau)\,d\sigma(x)\,d\tau.

Here we have chosen
v(0)=0
by subtracting a suitable constant from the solution
u
. Since
g\in L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
, this implies that
g\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R},L^{1}(\partial\mathcal{O}))
, the right-hand side integral is finite for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and hence
v
is a continuous function on
\mathbb{R}
. Here we use the fact that for a bounded Lipschitz domain
L^{p}(\partial\mathcal{O})\subset L^{1}(\partial\mathcal{O})
.

Suppose now that (
A.2
) holds. We then have
\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\in L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
and hence for
d=\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{O})

\iint_{\Omega}|D^{1/2}_{t}u|^{p}dX\,dt\leq C(d)\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}^{p}<\infty.

Hence
D^{1/2}_{t}u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
and therefore also

D^{1/2}_{t}u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R},L^{1}(\mathcal{O}))
, this time using

L^{p}(\mathcal{O})\subset L^{1}(\mathcal{O})
for the bounded set
\mathcal{O}
. Hence

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|D^{1/2}_{t}v(t)\right|^{p}\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|D^{1/2}_{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}}u\,dX\right|^{p}\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\int_{\mathcal{O}}D^{1/2}_{t}u\,dX\right|^{p}\,dt<\infty.

Thus we must have
D^{1/2}_{t}v\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
. We shall find
g\in L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
for which

D^{1/2}_{t}v\notin L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
thus contradicting our original claim that (
A.2
) holds.

Consider first a nonnegative, even “bump” function
g
that depends only on the time variable and has support in
[-1,1]
. Clearly, for such
g
we have
g\in L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
.
Furthermore, assume that it is normalized so that

\sigma(\partial\mathcal{O})\int_{-1}^{1}g(\tau)\,d\tau=2.

Then for
v
defined as above, since
g
is an even function,
v
must be an odd function (
v(-t)=-v(t)
). Also,
v(t)=1
for all
t\geq 1
, due to our conditions on
g
. We conclude that
v
can be decomposed into the sign function and a compactly supported “defect” function
E(t)
:

v(t)=\operatorname{sgn}(t)+E(t),

where
E(t)=v(t)-\operatorname{sgn}(t)
. By the analysis above,
E(t)
is exactly zero for
|t|\geq 1
. Because
v(t)
and
\operatorname{sgn}(t)
are odd,
E(t)
is an odd function supported in
[-1,1]
.

By linearity of the fractional derivative:

D^{1/2}_{t}v(t)=D^{1/2}_{t}\operatorname{sgn}(t)+D^{1/2}_{t}E(t).

Applying the global fractional derivative operator to the sign function yields the exact leading term:

D^{1/2}_{t}\operatorname{sgn}(t)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\operatorname{sgn}(t)|t|^{-1/2},

where for points outside the support (
|t|\gg 1
), the operator
D^{1/2}_{t}
acting on the compactly supported
E(t)
takes the form of a convolution integral with a kernel proportional to
|t-\tau|^{-3/2}
. Analyzing this yields
that for
|t|\geq 1
, the asymptotic expansion of the half-derivative is cleanly given by:

D^{1/2}_{t}v(t)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\operatorname{sgn}(t)|t|^{-1/2}+\mathcal{O}(|t|^{-5/2}).

Hence clearly,
D^{1/2}_{t}v\notin L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
for all
1\leq p\leq 2
. To find a counterexample when
p>2
we consider instead, for a fixed parameter
\alpha\in(-1,0)
, the slowly decaying function

g(t)=\sigma(\partial\mathcal{O})^{-1}(1+t^{2})^{\alpha/2},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

Clearly,
g\in L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
for all
\alpha<-1/p
, i.e.,
p>-1/\alpha
. Again let

v(t)=\int_{0}^{t}(1+\tau^{2})^{\alpha/2}\,d\tau.

As
|t|\to\infty
, the function
v(t)
separates into a leading divergent power-law term, a constant correction (from the convergent tail), and a decaying error term. The asymptotic expansion is:

v(t)=\operatorname{sgn}(t)\frac{|t|^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}+C(\alpha)\operatorname{sgn}(t)+\mathcal{O}(|t|^{\alpha-1}).

The asymptotic behavior of the half-derivative
D^{1/2}_{t}v(t)
is as follows. For large
|t|
, the expansion is:

D^{1/2}_{t}v(t)=A_{1}\operatorname{sgn}(t)|t|^{\alpha+1/2}+A_{2}\operatorname{sgn}(t)|t|^{-1/2}+\mathcal{O}(|t|^{\alpha-3/2}),

where (just for the curiosity of an interested reader) the coefficients are given by:

A_{1}=-\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)\sin\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\alpha+3/2)\cos\left(\frac{\pi(2\alpha+1)}{4}\right)}>0,\quad A_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha+1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}<0.

The case
\alpha=-1/2
is particularly clean since
at this critical threshold, the exponent of the leading term becomes exactly zero. Hence the asymptotic expansion transitions into a
step function
:

D^{1/2}_{t}v(t)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\operatorname{sgn}(t)+A_{2}\operatorname{sgn}(t)|t|^{-1/2}+\mathcal{O}(|t|^{-2}).

It follows that
D^{1/2}_{t}v\notin L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
for all
p<\infty
. This therefore extends the counterexample to all
2<p<\infty
. The case
p=\infty
can also be excluded by considering
\alpha\in(-1/2,0)
. It follows that
(
A.2
) fails to hold for bounded
\mathcal{O}
for all
1<p\leq\infty
as claimed.∎

We note that the intuitive reason the bound (
A.2
) does fail for all
p>1
is that
\int_{\partial\Omega}g
does not have to be zero; in fact, in our examples above
g
was always nonnegative. It then follows that with such
g

the flux flows into the domain and the total “heat” at a given time
t
is
v(t)
, which can only increase
as can be seen from (
A.3
). Clearly for
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
datum
g
when
p>1
, imposing the condition

\int_{\partial\Omega}g=0
would not make sense since the domain is unbounded in time and hence the spaces
L^{1}(\partial\Omega)
and
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
are not contained in each other (and therefore an
L^{p}
function is not necessarily an
L^{1}
one, which any condition on
\int_{\partial\Omega}g
would require). The situation is different for
p=1
, when
it is possible to consider
g
in a Hardy space
\hbar^{1}_{\mathrm{ato}}(\partial\Omega)
built from “atoms”
that come with a mean-zero condition. We shall leave it to the reader to see that the proof we give below in the unbounded case can then be modified and
L^{1}
bounds similar to (
A.6
) can be obtained on a domain
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
with bounded
\mathcal{O}
.

A.2.
The graph domain case
\mathcal{O}=\{x_{n}>\phi(x)\}
(unbounded)

We now consider the case when the domain
\mathcal{O}
is unbounded in space. Unlike the bounded case considered above, we expect the “heat” added to the system at a given time by the function
g
to escape to spatial infinity with increasing time,
and therefore even with
\int_{\partial\Omega}g\neq 0
or
g\geq 0
we shall have
u(\cdot,t)\to 0
for both
t\to\pm\infty
. Recall that the fact that the limits of
u
(or more precisely of its integral
v(t)
over
\mathcal{O}
) at
t\to-\infty
and
t\to\infty
disagreed was the main reason our counterexamples given above do work.

We start by considering the case
p=2
.
Via the pullback, the case where
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
with
\mathcal{O}
being an unbounded Lipschitz graph domain (that is, of the form (
2.3
)) with sufficiently small Lipschitz constant follows from a standard bi-Lipschitz change of variables and therefore it suffices to consider the case
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Clearly, if we establish that for an energy solution of
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
with Neumann datum
g\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega)\cap\dot{H}^{-1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
we have an estimate

(A.4)

\|D^{1/2}_{t}u\big|_{\partial\Omega}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}+\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},

the result for
p=2
would follow from the estimate
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}
, which is just
L^{2}
Neumann problem solvability from Theorem
1.6
,
the estimate
S\lesssim N
of Theorem
7.1

and (
A.1
) giving us that

(A.5)

\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}+\|\tilde{N}(H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},

as desired.

For simplicity of notation let
w=D^{1/2}_{t}u
. We write, after turning the boundary integral into a solid one and integrating by parts:

\int_{\partial\Omega}w^{2}\,dx\,dt=-2\iint_{\Omega}(\partial_{n}w)\,w\,dX\,dt=-2\iint_{\Omega}w\,D^{1/2}_{t}(\partial_{n}u)(\partial_{n}x_{n})\,dX\,dt\\
=2\iint_{\Omega}(\partial_{n}w)D^{1/2}_{t}(\partial_{n}u)x_{n}\,dX\,dt+2\iint_{\Omega}(D^{1/2}_{t}w)(\partial^{2}_{nn}u)x_{n}\,dX\,dt=:A_{1}+A_{2}.

We note here that on a purely technical level we should integrate in the variable
x_{n}
only on an interval
(0,h)
and then let
h\to\infty
, but that does not impact the calculation in any way and hence we took the liberty to skip this step. Integration over a finite interval would yield an additional boundary term or an interior solid term
like in (
6.23
) of the type
\int_{h/2}^{h}\int_{\partial\Omega}|w|^{2}\,dX\,dt
if we use a cutoff function instead. For energy solutions
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
we have
D^{1/2}_{t}u=w\in L^{2}(\Omega)

and therefore in the limit such a term would disappear.

Term
A_{2}
has a rather simple estimate using the fact that

D^{1/2}_{t}w=D^{1/2}_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}u=-H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}u=-H_{t}\partial_{t}u=-H_{t}\divg(A\nabla u),

thanks to the PDE
u
satisfies. After distributing the derivative
|\divg(A\nabla u)|\sim|\nabla^{2}u|+|\nabla A||\nabla u|
we have by Cauchy-Schwarz that

|A_{2}|\lesssim\left(\iint_{\Omega}\left[|\nabla^{2}u|+|\nabla A||\nabla u|\right]^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}\\
\lesssim\|S(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}+\|\mu\|_{C}\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},

where for the last term we have used the Carleson condition on coefficients (
1.3
) and the Hilbert transform
H_{t}
has been eliminated since it is
L^{2}
bounded.

For the term
A_{1}
we introduce an additional
x_{n}
into the integral and integrate by parts:

A_{1}=\iint_{\Omega}(\partial_{n}w)D^{1/2}_{t}\left(\partial_{n}u\right)\partial_{n}(x_{n}^{2})\,dX\,dt\\
=-\iint_{\Omega}(\partial^{2}_{nn}u)D^{1/2}_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}\left(\partial_{n}u\right)x_{n}^{2}\,dX\,dt-\iint_{\Omega}(\partial_{n}D^{1/2}_{t}w)(\partial^{2}_{nn}u)x_{n}^{2}\,dX\,dt=:2A_{11},

where we have freely shifted
D^{1/2}_{t}
from one factor to the other (using its self-adjointness) as required. Again, since
D^{1/2}_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}=-H_{t}\partial_{t}
we get that

|A_{11}|\lesssim\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}\|A(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}\left(\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\mu\|^{1/2}_{C}\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
\lesssim\|S(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}+\|\mu\|_{C}\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},

where the area function
A
is as in (
2.18
) and (
2.20
) was used in the penultimate step.

From this we obtain (
A.4
), since all terms enjoy
estimates in terms of
\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}
and
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}
. This establishes the
L^{2}

bounds in (
A.5
) that we were seeking.

Clearly, the calculation we have just finished is
L^{2}
-specific and does not extend to
L^{p}
for
p\neq 2
, as manipulation with
D^{1/2}_{t}
becomes impossible. To extend these bounds to
1<p\leq 2
, interpolation is required. At this point we recall some results of the paper
[
15
]
, where in the last section we introduced an interpolation result for the bound on
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}(\partial\Omega)}
under the assumption that we have
L^{p}
Neumann solvability. Here
1<q\leq p
.

We quickly recall what is needed.
We assume that the
L^{2}
Neumann problem for
\mathcal{L}
and the
L^{2}
Dirichlet problem for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
are solvable.
We shall establish the end-point
p=1
Hardy space bound of the form

(A.6)

\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C,

for all
u
that solve
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
with Neumann boundary data
g
, where
g
is any

L^{\infty}
atom, i.e.,

\supp g\subset Q_{r}(X,t)\cap\partial\Omega\quad\mbox{for some $(X,t)\in\partial\Omega$ and $r>0$},\quad\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq r^{-n-1},\quad\int_{\partial\Omega}g=0.

This bound implies solvability of the Neumann boundary value problem for
p=1
with Hardy space data. Then by interpolation we get, for all
1<q\leq 2
, that the sublinear operator

T:g\mapsto\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)

is
L^{q}(\partial\Omega)
bounded, using the real interpolation method. Thanks to (
A.6
) we know that
T
is bounded from
\hbar^{1}_{\mathrm{ato}}(\partial\Omega)\to L^{1}(\partial\Omega)
, where
\hbar^{1}_{\mathrm{ato}}(\partial\Omega)
is the atomic
Hardy space consisting of functions of the form
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}g_{i}
such that
\sum_{i}|\lambda_{i}|<\infty
and each
g_{i}
is an
L^{\infty}
atom as defined above.
T
is also bounded from
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
to
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
as we established above in (
A.5
). It then follows that
T
is bounded as an operator on
L^{q}(\partial\Omega)
for all
1<q\leq 2
.

Following Brown
[
4
]
,
by rescaling and translation, we may assume that the atom
g
is supported in a parabolic boundary cube

Q_{1}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega
and therefore has zero average and
|g|\leq 1
.
Let
u
be the energy solution of the Neumann boundary value problem in
\Omega
with datum
g
.
We aim to prove (
A.6
) for some
C>0
independent of
g
. As in
[
15
]
we may consider an even reflection
\tilde{u}
across the boundary
which extends
u
to the set
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\setminus\overline{Q_{1+\varepsilon}(0,0)}
. This extension
\tilde{u}
solves on this domain a parabolic PDE

\partial_{t}\tilde{u}-{\mathrm{div}\,}(\tilde{A}\nabla\tilde{u})=0,

where
\tilde{A}=A
on
\Omega
and the coefficient matrix
\tilde{A}
on the set
\{x_{n}<0\}
only depends on
A(x,-x_{n},t)
, making the resulting matrix bounded and uniformly elliptic on the set
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\setminus\overline{Q_{1+\varepsilon}(0,0)}
.
We have shown in
[
15
]
that
\tilde{u}
satisfies the bound

(A.7)

|\tilde{u}(X,t)|\lesssim\|(X,t)\|^{-n-\eta},\qquad\mbox{for all $(X,t)$ with }\|(X,t)\|\geq 2,

for some small
\eta>0
.

Proof of (
A.6
).

We start with estimating
\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)
in a neighborhood of
Q_{1}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega
.
By Hölder’s inequality and (
A.5
) we have that

(A.8)

\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{1}(Q_{16}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega)}\leq|Q_{16}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega|^{1/2}\left(\int_{Q_{16}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega}\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)^{2}d\sigma\right)^{1/2}\\
\lesssim|Q_{16}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega|^{1/2}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega}|g|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq|Q_{16}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega|\leq C,

since the surface measure of a parabolic cube of size
16
on a Lipschitz graph is bounded by a uniform constant that only depends on the Lipschitz constant.

Consider now a sequence of annuli
S_{k}
partitioning the complement of the set
Q_{16}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega
in
\partial\Omega
. That is let

S_{k}=\{(x,t)\in\partial\Omega:2^{k}<\|(x,t)\|\leq 2^{k+1}\},\qquad\mbox{for }k=4,5,\dots.

Clearly, since the surface measure of
S_{k}
is proportional to
2^{k(n+1)}
, there exist constants
c>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, independent of
k
and depending only on
n
and the Lipschitz norm of
\phi
, such that:

•

For
r_{k}=c2^{k}
there exists a cover of
S_{k}
by
N
parabolic boundary balls
B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}

of the same size
r_{k}
,
j=1,2,\dots,N
.

•

d(B_{8r_{k}}^{k,j},Q_{1}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega)\geq c2^{k}
.

Recall that
D^{1/2}_{t}u(X,t)
is defined as
an integral involving the values
u(X,\tau)
for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, and hence it is possible that even if a point
(X,t)
has distance
\geq c2^{k}
to
Q_{1}(0,0)
, there exists some
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
for which the point
(X,\tau)
fails to satisfy this. Hence we have to consider this additional complication. For this reason let us split the nontangential cone into two parts.

For
(x,t)\in B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}
let
\Gamma(x,t)
be the corresponding nontangential cone. We write

\Gamma(x,t)=\Gamma^{\mathrm{good}}(x,t)\cup\Gamma^{\mathrm{bad}}(x,t),

where
(Y,s)\in\Gamma^{\mathrm{bad}}(x,t)
if for some
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
we have
d((Y,\tau),Q_{1}(0,0))<(c/4)2^{k}
. Otherwise
(Y,s)\in\Gamma^{\mathrm{good}}(x,t)
.

Take first
(Y,s)\in\Gamma^{\mathrm{good}}(x,t)
for
(x,t)\in B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}
. Let us estimate

\left(\fint_{W(Y,s)}|D^{1/2}_{t}u|^{2}dY^{\prime}ds^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}.

Here
W(Y,s)
is a Whitney box centered around
(Y,s)
. Let
W^{*}
be a small enlargement of this box.
A calculation as in
[
8
]
(cf. Section 3) implies that we have

(A.9)

|W|^{-1/2}\|D^{1/2}_{t}u\|_{L^{2}(W)}\lesssim|W|^{-1/2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(W^{*})}+\mathrm{Tail}(u;W),

where
\mathrm{Tail}(u;W)
is the contribution from
u
supported away from
W
. Since we have
(
A.7
), a simple calculation shows that

|\mathrm{Tail}(u;W)|\lesssim Cr_{k}^{-(n+1)-\eta}
and this estimate holds pointwise, i.e., not just in the
L^{2}
average sense. Hence

w_{1}(x,t):=\sup_{(Y,s)\in\Gamma^{\mathrm{good}}(x,t)}|W(Y,s)|^{-1/2}\left(\iint_{W(Y,s)}|D^{1/2}_{t}u|^{2}dY^{\prime}ds^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\tilde{N}(\nabla u)(x,t)+r_{k}^{-(n+1)-\eta}.

It follows that

\|w_{1}\|_{L^{1}(B_{r_{k}}^{k,j})}\leq C\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{1}(B_{r_{k}}^{k,j})}+Cr_{k}^{n+1}r_{k}^{-(n+1)-\eta}=Cr_{k}^{-\eta},

since the estimate for
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{1}(B_{r_{k}}^{k,j})}
is established in
[
15
]
.
To obtain an
L^{1}
bound on each
S_{k}
we sum over
j
to get

\|w_{1}\|_{L^{1}(S_{k})}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\|w_{1}\|_{L^{1}(B_{r_{k}}^{k,j})}\lesssim r_{k}^{-\eta}.

It remains to consider
(Y,s)\in\Gamma^{\mathrm{bad}}(x,t)
for
(x,t)\in B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}
, where there are points
(Y,s^{\prime})
at distance to
Q_{1}(0,0)
less than
(c/4)2^{k}
.
This only happens for parabolic balls
B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}
in specific locations (see Figure
1
) whose center is near the
t
-axis in the boundary
(x,t)
coordinates. Consider one such ball.

t
x
Cube at
(0,0)
Region
R_{1}
Region
R_{2}
Region
R^{\prime}

Figure 1.
Splitting of a parabolic ball into regions based on their distance to
t
-axis on the boundary
\partial\Omega
.

We write

B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}=\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}R_{i}\right)\cup R^{\prime},\quad\mbox{where:}\quad R_{i}=\begin{cases}B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}\cap\{(x,t):|x|<2\},&\quad i=1,\\
B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}\cap\{(x,t):2^{i-1}\leq|x|<2^{i}\},&\quad i>1,\end{cases}

where
R^{\prime}
is the rest of the set. For
(x,t)\in R^{\prime}
nothing changes and the estimate we gave for
w_{1}(x,t)

applies to the whole cone
\Gamma(x,t)
.

Next consider
(x,t)\in R_{i}
for
1<i\leq k
. For any
(Y,s)\in\Gamma^{\mathrm{bad}}(x,t)
, the line

\{(Y,s^{\prime}):s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\}
misses
Q_{1}(0,0)
and the minimal distance between this line and
Q_{1}(0,0)

is
\sim 2^{i}
. Let us calculate the contribution of such far points to
D^{1/2}_{t}u(Y,s)
.
This will be at most

(A.10)

\sum_{j=i}^{k}\int_{|s^{\prime}|\sim 2^{2j}}\frac{|u(Y,s)-u(Y,s^{\prime})|}{|s-s^{\prime}|^{3/2}}ds^{\prime}\lesssim\sum_{j=i}^{k}\int_{|s^{\prime}|\sim 2^{2j}}\frac{2^{-j(n+\eta)}}{(r_{k}^{2})^{3/2}}ds^{\prime}\\
\lesssim r_{k}^{-3}\sum_{j=i}^{k}2^{-j(n-2+\eta)}\lesssim r_{k}^{-3}2^{-i(n-2+\eta)},

using (
A.7
). This is an extra correction term that needs to be added to
w_{1}
when
(x,t)\in R_{i}
. It therefore follows that
for
(x,t)\in R_{i}
,
i>1
, we have

w_{2}(x,t):=\sup_{(Y,s)\in\Gamma^{\mathrm{bad}}(x,t)}|W(Y,s)|^{-1/2}\left(\iint_{W(Y,s)}|D^{1/2}_{t}u|^{2}dY^{\prime}ds^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}\\
\lesssim\tilde{N}(\nabla u)(x,t)+r_{k}^{-(n+1)-\eta}+r_{k}^{-3}2^{-i(n-2+\eta)}.

We integrate over
R_{i}
, which is a region of diameter at most
2^{i}
in
x
-variables and
r_{k}^{2}=2^{2k}
in the time variable. This gives

(A.11)

\|w_{2}\|_{L^{1}(B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}\cap\partial\Omega)}\leq Cr_{k}^{-\eta}+Cr_{k}^{-3}\sum_{i=2}^{k}2^{-i(n-2+\eta)}2^{i(n-1)}r_{k}^{2}+\mbox{contribution from $R_{1}$},

where the first term
Cr_{k}^{-\eta}
arises in the same way as in
w_{1}
, with the middle term enjoying the estimate

r_{k}^{-3}\sum_{i=2}^{k}2^{-i(n-2+\eta)}2^{i(n-1)}r_{k}^{2}\lesssim r_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i=2}^{k}2^{i(1-\eta)}\sim r_{k}^{-1}2^{k(1-\eta)}\sim r_{k}^{-1}r_{k}^{1-\eta}=r_{k}^{-\eta}.

Hence this term is of the same size as the main term and thus will not cause any issues. It remains to account for the possible contribution from
(x,t)\in R_{1}
, where for some points
(Y,s)\in\Gamma^{\mathrm{bad}}(x,t)

the line
\{(Y,s^{\prime}):s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\}
passes through
Q_{1}(0,0)
.
We can “cheat” and go around the ball
Q_{1}(0,0)
while maintaining a distance of at least

O(1)
to it.

Recalling (
A.9
), let us write

(A.12)

|W|^{-1/2}\|D^{1/2}_{t}u\|_{L^{2}(W)}\lesssim|W|^{-1/2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(W)}+\mathrm{Tail}(v;W)+\mathrm{Tail}(u-v;W),

where
v(Y,s^{\prime})=u(Y,s^{\prime})
if
d((Y,s^{\prime}),Q_{1}(0,0))\geq 1
and

v(y,y_{n},s^{\prime})=u(y,y_{n}+[1-d((Y,s^{\prime}),Q_{1}(0,0))],s^{\prime}),\qquad\mbox{otherwise}.

Then
|W|^{-1/2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(W)}+\mathrm{Tail}(v;W)
enjoys estimates identical to those of
w_{2}
in regions
R_{i}
,
i>1
,
since we may use (
A.7
) for
v
and hence the calculation remains the same as the one above.

Of course this means we still need to estimate
\mathrm{Tail}(u-v;W)
. Using the same methodology as
for the term (
A.10
) we get an error of size

(A.13)

\int_{|s^{\prime}|\sim O(1)}\frac{|u(Y,s^{\prime})-v(Y,s^{\prime})|}{|s-s^{\prime}|^{3/2}}ds^{\prime}\lesssim r_{k}^{-3}N(\partial_{n}u)(y,\tau),

since for the difference

u-v
we use the fundamental theorem of calculus. Here
N(\partial_{n}u)(y,\tau)
is the nontangential maximal function at a good point inside
Q_{1}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega
. As we
L^{2}
average over a Whitney cube
W
, this can be replaced by
\tilde{N}(\partial_{n}u)
. Finally, the region
R_{1}
has diameter at most
O(1)
in the
x
-variable and

r_{k}^{2}
in time. This yields that the last term in (
A.11
) can be estimated by

\mbox{contribution from $R_{1}$}\lesssim r_{k}^{-3}\left(\fint_{Q_{1}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega}\tilde{N}(\partial_{n}u)(y,\tau)\,d\sigma(y)\,d\tau\right)r_{k}^{2}\times O(1)^{n-1}\sim r_{k}^{-1}.

Here we have used that
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C
for an atom (proven in
[
15
]
). We see that this term enjoys better decay than the other two terms in (
A.11
), so that

\|w_{2}\|_{L^{1}(B_{r_{k}}^{k,j}\cap\partial\Omega)}\lesssim r_{k}^{-\eta}+r_{k}^{-\eta}+r_{k}^{-1}\lesssim r_{k}^{-\eta}
.
The two estimates for
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
together with (
A.8
) then imply that

\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega)}\leq\|\tilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{1}(Q_{16}(0,0)\cap\partial\Omega)}+\sum_{k\geq 4}[\|w_{1}\|_{L^{1}(S_{k})}+\|w_{2}\|_{L^{1}(S_{k})}]\\
\leq C+C\sum_{k\geq 4}2^{-k\eta}\leq\tilde{C}<\infty.

Hence claim (
A.6
) follows.
∎

We have not resolved what happens for
p>2
, this case clearly requires a different approach.
